| `dicom_pull_jobs` | `(status, created_at)` | Missing today; the pull-job worklist query currently has no covering index |

No index is added speculatively. `usg_measurements` (~200 columns, per the DB map) is a column-list-only table by convention (never `SELECT *`) rather than an indexing problem — the fix there is query discipline, not a new index, and is out of this chapter's scope.

### Future migrations

**The two sanctioned paths, applied.** Every migration below goes through exactly one of the two D-36 paths — Drizzle files run by `care-migrate` (typed schema, used when application code needs typed access to new columns/tables), or idempotent feature migrations in `migrations/*.sql` registered in entrypoint Step 5 (used for constraint/index-only changes, or additive column patches that don't need immediate typed access). Neither path is `runStartupMigrations()` (frozen, no new tables) and neither is `drizzle-kit push` in any form, in any environment (§0.7). A third, narrower queue exists for genuinely dangerous operations — anything requiring an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` table rewrite, a `DROP`, or touching audit/backup mechanics — which lands in `migrations/manual-only/` and awaits explicit approval before it is even drafted for one of the two paths; this queue is not a third *sanctioned* path, it is a holding area that feeds into path (2) only after sign-off.

| Order | Migration | Path | Depends on | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Entrypoint fail-closed fix: `ON_ERROR_STOP=1` for feature migrations, Step-5 registration order authoritative (alphabetical glob removed), `add_performance_indexes.sql` registered | Feature (entrypoint script edit, not a `.sql` file itself) | none | Prerequisite for trusting every migration below — D-36 |
| 2 | `radiology_catalog_entries`/`_libraries`/`_aliases`/`_combos`/`_templates` + the two catalog indexes above | Drizzle | #1 | Chapter 1's first build-order step; everything else in this chapter that references catalog vocabulary follows this |
| 3 | `audit_logs` hardening: unique index on chain hash, revoking UPDATE/DELETE privileges (or an equivalent BEFORE trigger), real verification endpoint, `bigint` PK conversion | Manual-only queue → Feature, maintenance window, pre-change `pg_dump` (D-35) | #1 | D-34's remaining C4 items; this register is the recorded approval (D-34) |
| 4 | `ai_suggestion_audit` + `audit_log_id` FK, bigint PK, the three indexes above | Drizzle | #3 (needs a stable, hardened chain to FK into) | Chapter 7 |
| 5 | `radiology_measurement_pool` + its indexes | Drizzle, behind `ff_radiology_measurement_pool` | #2 (loose — logical `meas.<key>` reference by value) | Chapter 5 |
| 6 | `radiology_analytics_events` + its indexes | Drizzle | #2, #4, #5 (its dimensions key off their vocabularies) | Chapter 14 consumes it; this chapter creates it |
| 7 | `pacs_settings` unique `(key, category)` | Feature | none | Backlog fix, low risk |
| 8 | `dicom_pull_jobs (status, created_at)` | Feature | none | Backlog fix, low risk |
| 9 | `feature_flags.branch_id` (nullable) | Drizzle (typed application read of `branch_id`) | none structurally; gated procedurally by Chapter 8's multi-center prerequisite | Do not build ahead of an actual second-site onboarding |
| 10 | Sentinel-gated partitioning of `report_finding_instances` (Strand N) | Manual-only queue → Drizzle/DDL, maintenance window | #2, #4, #5 largely landed and stable | See Partitioning; never bundled with any other migration (B-23) |
| 11 | Draft-purge maintenance job | Not a schema migration — an ops script | Chapter 2's state machine live in production | See Archival |

**Idempotency, restated once for this chapter's own migrations specifically.** Every file above follows the §0.7 rules verbatim: tables, columns, and indexes are created only if not already present; seed rows are inserted only if an equivalent row is not already present, with a conflicting row skipped rather than overwritten; no migration ever drops or truncates a table, or issues an unfiltered delete; and no column becomes mandatory without a default on a table that already holds rows. Every file is safe to run ten times in a row, and no filename is ever reused. New tables (items 2, 4, 5, 6 above) are the one context where a mandatory column may ship without a default — inside the table's initial creation, on a table that ships empty (A1 pattern) — because there are zero existing rows to violate the constraint.

**Production-restore reconciliation, and why it is not a template for shipping new tables.** Restoring a Postgres volume brings back `schema_migrations_log`/`drizzle.__drizzle_migrations` from the backup, so subsequent migrations skip as "already applied" even when the restored data predates them, and `care-schema-verify` reports `full_fail`. The fix is `node scripts/generate-schema-reconcile.cjs --date YYYYMMDD`, which writes a new `migrations/zz_schema_reconcile_<date>.sql` — every statement wrapped in a `DO` block that downgrades a failure to a warning, so a restore-induced gap can never block a deploy. Two rules bound this mechanism, both binding: (1) it is **reserved exclusively for restore-induced drift** — a genuinely new table or column ships through path (1) or (2) above, never by hand-adding it to a reconcile file, because the warning-downgrade behavior that makes reconcile safe for *drift* would silently mask a *real* migration failure if the two purposes were mixed; (2) re-reconciling after a second restore means **deleting the old `zz_schema_reconcile_<date>.sql` and regenerating with a new date** — the entrypoint never re-applies a changed file under an unchanged name, so an edited-in-place reconcile file is invisible to the next deploy. Every schema-review checklist for this chapter's migrations must confirm the change came from path (1)/(2), not from a reconcile file, before merge.

### Partitioning

**What grows unbounded.** Four tables grow at a rate proportional to reporting volume, not to patient count, and are the partitioning candidates: `report_finding_instances` (every fired tile, every AI-accepted instance, at 10M+ structured findings under the platform's stated mandate), `ai_suggestion_audit` (unconditional per-rendered-suggestion recording — every ghost, card, and gutter mark, accepted or not, B-10), `radiology_analytics_events` (every state transition across four content chapters), and `audit_logs` (every clinical and admin action, hash-chained). `patient_reports` and `radiology_catalog_*` are explicitly **not** partitioning candidates: one grows at one row per signed study (orders of magnitude smaller than its findings), the other is small, governed content with a low, slow-growing row count.

**The sentinel (decided here — no source document specifies a number).** Partitioning is triggered per table when it crosses **20 million rows or 20 GB on-disk size, whichever comes first**, checked monthly against `pg_stat_user_tables`/`pg_total_relation_size` and reported alongside the other production health metrics (Chapter 13's deployment verification, Chapter 14's dashboards). Below this threshold, the operational complexity partitioning adds — per-partition constraint maintenance, index rebuilds, more moving parts in the backup/restore story — is not justified on a single Synology NAS; this is the concrete instrument for Strand N's flag (`ff_radiology_scale_partition`), which is explicitly **not** part of the clinical rollout ladder and must never be scheduled by calendar time or reading-day counts (Chapter 8, §8.4).

**Partition key and granularity.** Range partitioning by `created_at` (or the table's equivalent timestamp — `rendered_at` for `ai_suggestion_audit`, `occurred_at` for `radiology_analytics_events`), monthly granularity. This is chosen for three reasons: it matches Postgres native declarative partitioning's proven scale envelope; it aligns exactly with the cold-storage tiering boundary defined in Archival, so a partition that ages out of the hot window is already a self-contained physical unit ready to move; and the dominant query patterns (per-study lookups via the existing `draft_id`/`report_id`/`finding_id`/`study_ref` indexes, and date-bounded dashboard rollups) both stay fast under monthly partitioning without needing the partition key in every query.

**Execution mechanics.** A monthly maintenance job pre-creates the next three months of partitions ahead of need — partitions are never created at insert time, to avoid DDL contention on the write path. No default/catch-all partition exists in steady state (every row must land in an explicit partition); a default partition is permitted only transiently, during the initial cutover, to catch any row racing the migration.

**Cutover procedure for an already-populated table (`report_finding_instances` is the first and hardest case).** Partitioning a live, populated table is not an in-place table rewrite. The procedure is: build the new partitioned table under a temporary name; backfill via a batched, ordered copy (rows selected and inserted in partition-key order, in bounded batches); rehearse the entire cutover against a **restored copy of production**, never against production directly; then, inside a maintenance window with a fresh `pg_dump` taken immediately beforehand (D-35), swap table names and re-point dependent sequences/indexes with a brief write-lock. This mirrors the same caution the CTO review gives for a live `int4`→`bigint` rewrite ("hours of downtime on NAS I/O") because the underlying constraint — a full-table rewrite on NAS-class disk I/O — is the same physical operation. This is a manual-only-queue migration by construction (not a routine feature migration), scheduled off the sentinel, never bundled with any other schema change (B-23).

**Read-replica routing — explicitly deferred, not scheduled by row count.** Strand N's flag description also names "read-replica routing," but a read replica is a distinct infrastructure investment (a second streaming-replication Postgres instance) that this chapter does not schedule against the partitioning sentinel. It is gated by demonstrated single-box I/O contention measured *after* partitioning has landed and been observed in production for at least one full measurement cycle — building replica-routing logic ahead of that evidence is premature infrastructure the single-clinic deployment does not yet need (risk-governance T3: today there is exactly one un-replicated Postgres, by design, at this scale).

**`audit_logs` is explicitly not partitioned in this plan.** The near-term prescription for `audit_logs` is the bigint PK conversion and the other D-34 hardening items (Future migrations, item 3) — not partitioning. The reason is structural, not a scheduling choice: Postgres requires a unique constraint enforced across partitions to include the partition key, and the audit hash chain's uniqueness guarantee (D-34's unique index on the chain hash) is a flat, global property of the chain, not one scoped by date. Partitioning `audit_logs` as currently designed would either weaken that uniqueness guarantee or require redesigning the chain itself to be partition-aware — neither is this chapter's decision to make unilaterally. This is flagged as requiring Chief Clinical Architect sign-off before any `audit_logs` partitioning is attempted, and it is out of this chapter's build order.

**Pruning.** For `report_finding_instances`, `ai_suggestion_audit`, and any partition that might contain a row referencing a signed report, "pruning" never means dropping the partition outright — it means moving the partition to cheaper storage (Archival, below), because any such row is permanently retained per D-19/B-01/Chapter 2's archive doctrine. `radiology_analytics_events` is the one candidate table where partitions genuinely age out and are dropped after aggregation, because it is pure telemetry with no D-19-style forever guarantee — its pruning policy is stated fully in Archival.

### Archival

**Signed report retention — indefinite, not windowed.** `patient_reports.body` is permanent by construction (D-19, B-01): no source document authorizes deleting a signed report, ever, and this chapter does not invent a retention ceiling where none was adjudicated. No purge, partition-drop, or storage-reclamation job may ever target a row that has `report_id` set on `report_finding_instances`, a row in `report_amendments`, or an `ai_suggestion_audit` row referenced by a signed report's provenance view — this is a hard exclusion list any archival job's `WHERE` clause must encode, not a policy an operator remembers. C-11's ≥7-year figure (matching Indian medico-legal report retention) is the **floor** this chapter adopts for the AI audit trail and, by extension, for every structured artifact behind a signed report — it is not a ceiling or a target deletion date for the report itself.

**Special-category exclusion.** Form-F / PC-PNDT obstetric records (flagged High-sensitivity PII, audits-memory §11) are additionally subject to the PC-PNDT Act's own record-keeping mandate and are never included in any purge, drop-partition, or cold-tier eligibility check regardless of age — this is a second, independent entry on the same exclusion list as "has `report_id` set," not a special case of it.

**Cold storage, sized for a single Synology NAS.** No object storage tier (S3-class) exists in this deployment, so "cold storage" means a second PostgreSQL tablespace on a separate, larger/cheaper NAS volume or share, not a different storage product. Partitions older than an **active window of 24 months** (decided here — chosen to comfortably cover Chapter 5's Follow-up Comparison delta-brief use case, which routinely compares against priors years apart, while still moving the bulk of aging data off the primary fast volume) are relocated, partition by partition, to the cold tablespace using Postgres's native tablespace-relocation mechanism. This is a storage-tier operation only: cold partitions remain fully queryable at their original table/index — a delta brief spanning more than 24 months must still resolve correctly — mirroring Chapter 2's framing that partitioning is "a physical storage change, never a semantic one." `radiology_catalog_*` and `patient_reports` never move to cold tablespaces; both are small enough, and `patient_reports` in particular must stay on primary fast storage since it is the table every read path resolves a signed report through.

**Backup vs. archive — two different retention tracks, not one.** D-35's streaming `pg_dump`/WAL pipeline to `/volume1/backup/` is **operational disaster recovery**: a rolling window sized for restore speed and recent-data recovery, not for legal retention, and it is explicitly not the mechanism this chapter relies on for the 7-year (or longer, for Form-F) legal floor. A separate, longer-horizon logical archive — an annual full `pg_dump` retained on a dedicated archive share (or off-box once one exists) and never rotated out of that retention window regardless of the operational backup's rolling schedule — satisfies the legal-retention requirement independently. Conflating "backup" and "archive" was exactly the confusion D-35 resolved for disaster-recovery source-of-truth; this chapter's addition is narrow: legal archival is sized in years, operational backup is sized in days, and neither one substitutes for the other.

**Draft purge — the policy Chapter 2 deferred here.** Draft-scoped `report_finding_instances` rows (`draft_id` set, `report_id` null) plus their owning `radiology_report_drafts` row are eligible for purge under exactly two conditions, and no others: (a) the draft was **never signed** and has had no `updated_at` activity for **90 days**; or (b) the draft **was signed** (has a `final_report_id`) and **30 days** have elapsed since the sign-time snapshot copy succeeded — a grace period retained specifically so a post-sign investigation can still inspect the original working rows Chapter 2 promises are "retained separately until draft purge." Purge runs as a scheduled, logged maintenance job, never a live user-facing delete endpoint, dry-run first in any new environment, and its `WHERE` clause is the same hard exclusion — never a row with `report_id` set — that every archival job in this chapter shares. This job has no interaction with partitioning or cold storage: it operates on the hot, un-partitioned present regardless of what partitioning strategy `report_finding_instances` is under.

### Acceptance criteria

- Every table created after this chapter's adoption has a `bigint` identity PK, nullable `tenant_id`/`branch_id`, and no `MAX(seq)+1` key generator — verified at schema review, not after merge (D-37).
- `radiology_catalog_*`, `radiology_measurement_pool`, `ai_suggestion_audit`, and `radiology_analytics_events` each exist, ship empty, and landed via one of the two D-36-sanctioned paths; none was added to `runStartupMigrations()`.
- No migration file in the repository invokes `drizzle-kit push` or `pushSchema`, in any script, in any environment.
- `add_performance_indexes.sql` is registered in entrypoint Step 5 and verified applied on production via `psql \d`; `pacs_settings (key, category)` and `dicom_pull_jobs (status, created_at)` indexes exist and are verified the same way.
- Feature migrations run with `ON_ERROR_STOP=1`; a half-applied feature migration fails the deploy, never records false success.
- The partitioning sentinel (20M rows / 20 GB) is checked monthly and its result is visible in the deployment or analytics dashboard an engineer actually looks at; `ff_radiology_scale_partition` has never been flipped by calendar date or reading-day count.
- No purge, drop-partition, or cold-tier job — draft purge or `radiology_analytics_events` pruning included — ever touches a row with `report_id` set on `report_finding_instances`, a `report_amendments` row, or a signed-report-referenced `ai_suggestion_audit` row; this is covered by a regression test on each job's query, not by code review alone.
- `audit_logs` has not been partitioned without a prior, explicit Chief Clinical Architect sign-off on a chain-design change.
- Every schema-review checklist for a new table or column confirms the change traveled through Drizzle-via-care-migrate or a registered `migrations/*.sql` feature file — never a hand-edited `zz_schema_reconcile_*` file, which is reserved for restore-induced drift only.
- Cold-tier partitions remain fully queryable after the tablespace relocation; a Follow-up Comparison delta brief spanning more than 24 months still resolves correctly against a cold-tiered partition.

### Build order

1. Land the D-36 entrypoint fixes (`ON_ERROR_STOP=1`, Step-5 registration order authoritative, `add_performance_indexes.sql` registered) — every other migration in this chapter depends on the deploy pipeline being trustworthy first.
2. `radiology_catalog_*` schema (Drizzle path) — Chapter 1's own build-order step 1; land this before any other new table in this chapter, since Chapters 1–7 eventually key vocabulary off it.
3. `audit_logs` hardening (unique chain-hash index, revoking UPDATE/DELETE privileges or an equivalent trigger, verification endpoint, bigint PK) as a manual-only-queue, maintenance-window migration with a pre-change `pg_dump` — this is the D-34 approval this register already grants, and it must land before `ai_suggestion_audit` can FK into a stable chain.
4. `ai_suggestion_audit` (Drizzle, FK into the now-hardened `audit_logs`) — Chapter 7's dependency.
5. `radiology_measurement_pool` (Drizzle, behind `ff_radiology_measurement_pool`) — Chapter 5's dependency.
6. `radiology_analytics_events` (Drizzle) — land after 2, 4, and 5 so its event dimensions have stable vocabularies (catalog keys, audit dispositions, pool sources) to key against.
7. Backlog index fixes (`pacs_settings`, `dicom_pull_jobs`) — low-risk, feature-migration path, land opportunistically alongside any of the above.
8. Provision the cold-tier tablespace (second Synology volume/share) — an infrastructure prerequisite that can be prepared independently of the partitioning sentinel ever firing.
9. Draft-purge maintenance job — depends on Chapter 2's finding-instance state machine being live in production; land as a dry-run-first scheduled job before enabling live deletes.
10. Partitioning cutover for `report_finding_instances`, and later `ai_suggestion_audit`/`radiology_analytics_events`, each independently once it crosses the sentinel — manual-only queue, maintenance window, rehearsed against a restored copy first; never bundled with any other schema change.
11. `feature_flags.branch_id` — gated procedurally by Chapter 8's multi-center prerequisite, not by this chapter's own sequencing; do not build ahead of an actual second-site onboarding.

Steps 1–2 are prerequisites for essentially every other chapter's database-facing acceptance criteria; step 3 is a patient-safety-adjacent prerequisite (a hardened audit chain) that should not be deferred behind feature work; steps 10–11 are explicitly *not* near-term work — they are correctly sequenced last because their own trigger conditions (a size sentinel, a second hospital site) have not yet occurred as of this handbook's adoption.

---

## Chapter 10 — API Evolution

The radiology API grew across four uncoordinated eras (workflow-era routes, the USG extraction pipeline, the report-generator consolidation, and the as-built structured-JSON dual-write) with no version prefix, no shared error envelope, and no pagination convention — the CTO review's §4.4/§4.7 findings on unpaginated catalog search and the fragmented finalize/amendment surface are symptoms of this, not isolated bugs. This chapter is the owning chapter for B-30 (the four independent version namespaces) and states in full the API-specific half of D-27 (no route deletions), D-18 (catalog_version stamping), D-19/D-20 (signed-report authority and state machine as they touch the write path), and D-33 (server-derived authorship). It fixes the API's shape prospectively, the same way Chapter 9 fixes the schema prospectively: nothing existing is deleted or redesigned, but every new endpoint from Chapters 1–7's lifecycles and every endpoint this chapter adds must conform to the Final shape defined in §10.3, and the legacy surface is retired on a schedule anchored to Chapter 8's flag rollout ladder. Endpoints are named inline for engineers to locate in the routes tree; no request or response bodies are specified here.

### 10.1 Current API surface (as-built)

This is the frozen baseline. Every endpoint below exists in production today, is not versioned (no `/v1/` prefix anywhere in the tree), and per D-27 none of them may be deleted — only de-navigated once superseded, per §10.4.

**Feature flags** (owning chapter: 8). `GET /api/feature-flags` (any authenticated staff), `PATCH /api/feature-flags/:key` (`requireAdminRole`; 404 on unknown key — the API cannot create a flag).

**Structured templates.** `GET /api/radiology/structured-report-templates` (filters: `modality` applied, `bodyPart` accepted but not applied to the query — C-16, a defect this chapter's Final error model closes), `GET /api/radiology/structured-report-templates/:id`, `POST /api/radiology/structured-report-templates`, `PATCH /api/radiology/structured-report-templates/:id`, `DELETE /api/radiology/structured-report-templates/:id` (`FULL_ACCESS_ROLES`; presets undeletable), `POST /api/radiology/structured-report-templates/seed` (admin; idempotent by `templateName`+`isPreset`). This is the authoritative structured-template store per D-29; `radiology_structured_templates` and the other legacy template families are frozen read-only and carry no live routes worth enumerating here.

**Report generator** (mount: `/api/radiology/report-generator`, `requireStaffAuth`). `GET /templates`, `GET /protocols`, `GET /protocols/:key`, `POST /voice-cleanup`, `POST /generate`, `POST /save-draft` (the endpoint that performs the A3.2 dual-write into `report_finding_instances` when `ff_radiology_structured_core` is on), `GET /structured-json-drift` (admin-only, `?draftId=` optional; `{enabled:false}` cleanly when the flag is off — no auto-repair wired, per C-15), `GET /drafts`, `GET /drafts/:id`, `GET|PUT|DELETE /key-images(/:id)`, `GET|POST|PUT|DELETE /macros(/:id)`, `GET|PUT /preferences`, `GET|POST /normal-snippets`, `GET|POST|DELETE /image-references(/:id)`, `GET|PUT /style-preferences`, `GET /lifecycle-log`, `POST /log-action`, `GET|POST|DELETE /spinal-measurements(/:id)`, `GET|POST|PUT|DELETE /smart-macros(/:id)`. The `macros`/`smart-macros` endpoints are canonical per D-25; the workflow-era `radiologist_macros` endpoints (`radiologyWorkflow.ts`) are legacy and take no new features.

**AI drafting**, three coexisting endpoints with a defined precedence per D-30: `POST /api/ai-reporting/query` (primary interactive path — resolves the `radiology_draft` task route: caller override → active task route → global default), `POST /api/internal/radiology/ai-draft` (PACS-driven background job hook against `ai_job_queue`), `POST /api/smart-radiology/ai-impressions` (legacy, frozen, no new callers).

**Quick Select / catalog search.** The catalog-search route under `radiologyQuickFindings.ts` ships the entire global catalog to the client and filters in memory (`.includes()`) — no `WHERE`/`LIMIT` server-side (CTO §4.4, LOW). This is Current-stage only; §10.2 and §10.3 replace it.

**Finalize and amendments** — the fragmented surface D-19/D-20 name directly. `POST /api/usg-reports/:id/finalize` is the live finalize path (per D-27, explicitly retained "until the Workspace sign pipeline replaces it with an explicit migration"). Two separate, non-unified amendment endpoints exist: `GET|POST /api/smart-radiology/amendments/:reportDraftId` and `POST /api/usg-reports/:id/amend`. This is the "3–4 amendment chains" the CTO review and D-19 flag; D-19 names `report_amendments` as the one logical chain the data must converge on, but the route surface has not yet converged — see §10.4.

**Measurement extraction.** `GET /api/usg-extraction/study/:studyInstanceUID` (5 s poll while a study is active), `PATCH /api/usg-extraction/measurements/:id/approve`, `POST /api/usg-extraction/extract`.

**Locking, previous reports, system/version.** `GET|…/api/radiology/studies/:id/lock` (heartbeat locking, backs `radiology_study_locks`, reused by Chapter 9's C-07 pre-stage lock semantics), `GET /api/patient-reports/:patientId/previous?currentStudyId=:id` (up to 5 priors, patientId-keyed per D-03/B-25). `GET /api/system/version`, `GET /api/system/version/short`, `GET /health`, `GET /api/health/schema` — these are app-version endpoints (B-30's first namespace) and are out of scope for catalog/report versioning; they are listed here only so engineers do not confuse them with `catalog_version`/`template_version`/`render_engine_version`.

**Grammar note on the Current surface.** Path casing is inconsistent (kebab-case mounts like `structured-report-templates` alongside camelCase route files like `radiologyQuickFindings.ts` whose mount path is itself kebab-cased by convention but whose siblings are not), nesting is inconsistent (`report-generator` nests eighteen sub-resources flatly under one mount; `usg-reports` and `smart-radiology` each carry their own amendment sub-resource), and no endpoint returns pagination metadata. None of this is fixed retroactively — Current stays as documented above until each piece is superseded per §10.4. It is fixed prospectively starting in §10.2.

### 10.2 Intermediate API surface (additive, for Chapters 1–7 while old clients still run)

Coexistence rule, stated once and binding for every item below: **every Intermediate-stage change is either a new endpoint or a new optional field on an existing endpoint's request or response.** No existing required field, status code, or response shape may change while any client not yet migrated to the new flag stage still calls the old shape. This is D-27 applied at the field level, not just the route level. A flag being off must be indistinguishable, from an old client's point of view, from the endpoint never having changed.

**Catalog import (Strand H / C-01, feeds `ff_radiology_catalog_delta`).** A new admin-only import-trigger endpoint and an admin-only catalog-listing endpoint (by prefix, for verification) are additive — nothing in the Current surface reads from the new `radiology_catalog_*` tables yet. `report_finding_instances.finding_id` keeps pointing at `radiology_quick_findings.id` until Strand H lands (per C-01); the import endpoint does not repoint existing rows. A catalog-version indicator (read endpoint, admin-visible) is new and additive. The versioned per-modality delta-sync endpoint that `ff_radiology_catalog_delta` gates is new; it coexists with the Current whole-catalog client fetch for as long as `ff_radiology_search_v2`/`catalog_delta` remain below hospital stage (§10.4 fires the cutover).

**Structured finding writes (Chapters 2, 5; flags C, D, E).** No new endpoint is required for the base write path — `POST .../save-draft` already dual-writes per A3.2 when `ff_radiology_structured_core` is on (B-22's replace-not-append, `findings !== undefined`-gated semantics apply unchanged). The measurement pool (flag C) needs new sub-resources under `report-generator` for pool CRUD and attach/detach — additive siblings to the existing `spinal-measurements` pattern. Modality expansion (flag E) and classification (flag D) add optional fields to existing finding-instance payloads (a `modality` discriminator, a `classification_scale` reference) — old clients that never send these fields get the pre-expansion behavior by omission-default, never by branching on a version number.

**Drift repair (C-15).** The existing `GET .../structured-json-drift` stays read-only. A new admin-invoked repair-trigger endpoint (POST, per-draft) that re-runs A4's `regenerateDraftStructuredJson` is additive; it must never be wired to a cron per C-15 — invocation is always an explicit admin action, logged.

**Search (Strand G, `ff_radiology_search_v2`).** A new server-side search endpoint with `WHERE`/`LIMIT` and — once `pg_trgm` GIN indexes land per C-18 — trigram-ranked matching replaces the client-side `.includes()` filter. The old whole-catalog fetch is not removed at this stage; it remains the fallback for any client not yet on `search_v2` (§10.4 governs when it is de-navigated).

**AI assist (flag M) and provenance (D-32).** No new drafting endpoint — `/api/ai-reporting/query` stays primary per D-30. `POST .../insert-to-report` gains additive fields to persist span offsets and a suggestion id (not just the existing boolean flip) — this is the fix D-32 mandates for the five `.catch(()=>{})` audit-swallow sites in `aiReporting.ts`; those sites are corrected to fail loudly (log + alert) rather than swallow, which is a behavior change on the *failure path only* and is therefore compatible with the coexistence rule (success-path shape is unchanged).

**Finalize RBAC and authorship (D-33).** `POST /api/usg-reports/:id/finalize` and both amendment endpoints are tightened in place: authorship becomes server-derived from the authenticated session (`subjectName`); any client-supplied author string in the request body is now silently ignored rather than trusted. `canFinalize`/`canApprove` are enforced at the route. This is a request-field behavior change, not a shape change — old clients that were sending an author string keep working, they simply stop having that string honored, which is a security fix, not a contract break; it does not need a deprecation cycle because the old behavior (trusting the client string) was never a documented, reliable contract.

**Version stamping at sign (D-18/C-02).** The same finalize endpoints gain a same-transaction write of `catalog_version`, `template_version`, and `render_engine_version` onto `patient_reports` alongside `structured_json`. Purely additive column writes; no request or response shape changes.

**Voice-structured (flag K) and hierarchy/presets (flags I, J).** New optional fields on existing draft/preference endpoints (a `dictation_verb` log field, a `hierarchy_parent_key` on finding-instance writes, a `preset_id` on template application) — same additive pattern, no new mounts required beyond what Chapter 8 already names as each flag's gated surface.

Each item above inherits its build sequencing from Chapter 8's tier ordering (§8.3); this chapter does not reorder that — it only specifies what each tier's endpoint-level change looks like once its turn comes.

### 10.3 Final API shape (target once every flag is on)

This is the shape all new work targets once the Intermediate stage's additive changes have accumulated. It is not a rewrite migration — it is what the accumulated Intermediate endpoints converge toward, and legacy endpoints are retired into it per §10.4's schedule, never replaced in place.

**Resource model.** Six top-level resources, chosen to match the entities Chapters 1–7 already treat as first-class, not invented anew:

| Resource | Backing store | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `studies` | `radiology_worklist` (operational key, D-28) | `dicom_studies` remains the PACS/DICOM identity; the worklist resource is what the API exposes |
| `reports` | `patient_reports` (D-19 authoritative store) | one resource per report; drafts are the resource in `DRAFT` state, not a separate top-level resource |
| `reports/{id}/findings` | `report_finding_instances` (source of truth, B-22) | sub-resource; never a top-level `/findings` collection — findings do not exist outside a report |
| `reports/{id}/amendments` | `report_amendments` (D-19's one chain) | sub-resource; this is where the two Current-stage amendment endpoints converge |
| `catalog/*` | `radiology_catalog_*` (Strand H tables, C-01) | read-mostly from the API's perspective; writes are importer-only, never a client PATCH |
| `templates`, `macros` | `structured_report_templates` + `ai_normal_report_templates`; `radiology_text_macros` + `radiology_smart_macros` | one unified read/write surface per family per D-29/D-25, fronting the two backing tables each |

`feature_flags` remains its own admin resource (Chapter 8 owns it fully; this chapter does not restate its contract).

**Versioning convention.** The API does not carry a URL version prefix (no `/v1/`, no `/v2/`) and none is introduced. Per D-27's no-deletion stance, evolution is additive-forever at the route level; per B-30, the four version namespaces (app version.json/build number, `catalog_version`, `template_version`, `render_engine_version`) already carry every notion of "version" this system needs, and none of them is an API contract version. A resource's *shape* version, where it matters (e.g. a report's rendering contract), is expressed through the stamped `render_engine_version`/`template_version` fields on that resource, not through the URL. This keeps the single-box operational story simple (adjudication principle 5) and matches what is already built.

**Pagination and filtering.** Two conventions, chosen by table growth trajectory (the CTO's 100M-report / 10M-structured-finding projection, §4.7, is the deciding factor):

1. **Keyset (cursor) pagination** is mandatory for any list endpoint over a table projected to exceed roughly 100k rows per deployment — `reports`, `reports/{id}/findings` history views, audit-adjacent lists. Cursor is an opaque token encoding `(created_at, id)`; offset pagination is banned on these endpoints because it degrades to sequential scans at scale (the same failure mode as the CTO's leading-wildcard `ILIKE` finding). Default page size 25, server-enforced max 100.
2. **Offset pagination** is permitted only for small, bounded lists that cannot realistically exceed a few thousand rows on a single site — `templates`, `macros`, `catalog` prefix listings, `feature_flags`. Same default/max sizes.

Filtering is a server-side, whitelisted-parameter model on every list endpoint: each endpoint documents its accepted filter parameters, and **any parameter not on that whitelist is a 400, never silently ignored.** This closes the C-16 defect class (accepted-but-unapplied `bodyPart` filter) permanently, not just for that one endpoint — an accepted-and-ignored filter parameter is, from this chapter forward, an implementation bug, not a documented gap.

**Error model.** One envelope for every non-2xx response: a human-readable `error` string, a stable machine-readable `code`, and an optional `details` array for structured, multi-item failures (e.g. a list of blocking lint reasons). Status-code taxonomy, decisive and exhaustive for this API:

| Status | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| 400 | Malformed request: missing required field, invalid enum value, unknown/non-whitelisted filter parameter | unrecognized `bodyPart` value |
| 401 | No or invalid session | expired staff session |
| 403 | Authenticated but lacking the specific permission bit | `canFinalize` false, `requireAdminRole` failed, `FULL_ACCESS_ROLES` failed |
| 404 | Resource id or catalog key does not exist | unknown `finding_id`, unknown flag key (PATCH cannot create) |
| 409 | State conflict | study locked by another user (existing 423-style conflict, standardized to 409), sign attempted on a non-`DRAFT` report, catalog import targeting a `catalog_version` older than what is loaded |
| 422 | Domain/clinical validation failure | sign-gate blocked by an unmet ✕ contradiction (Master Spec §13.2), an importer B-16 validation failure, a scale↔management binding violation (D-16) |
| 500 | Unexpected server error | must always be logged with a request id; never a bare `.catch(()=>{})` swallow (the D-32 fix generalized as a standing rule for every route, not only AI audit writes) |

422 responses for sign-gate blocks always populate `details` with one entry per blocking reason, each carrying the same jump-link identifiers the reporting UI's gutter marks use — the API and the UI read the same blocking-reason vocabulary, never two.

**Idempotency rules — import.** A catalog import request's identity is the pack's content hash plus its declared `catalog_version`. Re-running an import whose content hash is unchanged and whose declared version is at or behind the currently-loaded `catalog_version` is a no-op that returns 200 with an "already applied" indicator — never a re-processing, never a duplicate governance event. A declared version *behind* the currently-loaded one is a hard 409 — the importer never silently downgrades a catalog. This is the concrete mechanism behind D-18's "bumped only by the pack importer" rule.

**Idempotency rules — sign.** The sign action (whichever endpoint fronts it, legacy `POST /api/usg-reports/:id/finalize` today or the Final `reports/{id}/sign` once §10.4 cuts it over) requires an `Idempotency-Key` header, generated once by the client when the sign gesture begins and reused on any automatic retry from that same gesture. Server behavior: a repeat request bearing a previously-seen key for the same report, whose draft content hash matches what was actually signed, returns 200 with the existing signed record — no second row in `patient_reports`, no second entry in `report_amendments`, no second version-stamping write. A repeat request with the same key but a *different* content hash (the draft changed between the original attempt and the retry) is a 409 — the server never silently signs the newer content under the old request's authority. A sign request with no `Idempotency-Key` at all is itself a 400 once this convention is in force — idempotency is not optional for an operation that freezes a legal document. This directly prevents the double-submit hazard inherent in D-19/D-20's append-only, one-way DRAFT→SIGNED transition: a network retry must never be able to produce two signed artifacts for one draft.

### 10.4 Deprecation plan

Deprecation in this system never means removal — D-27 forbids route deletion permanently. "Deprecated" means: annotated in the API inventory, de-navigated from new-client code paths, and left answering with correct data indefinitely for any caller that still reaches it directly (old integrations, audit tooling, direct scripts). The schedule below is anchored to Chapter 8's five-stage rollout ladder (internal → pilot → department → hospital → multi-center) so that "when do we deprecate X" is never a separate judgment call from "when is the replacing flag proven."

**Deprecation signalling.** Three checkpoints, in order, for every legacy endpoint being superseded:

1. **Department checkpoint.** The moment the successor flag reaches department stage (Chapter 8 §8.4: 10 reading days, TAT not regressed), the legacy endpoint is annotated `deprecated` in the API inventory, naming its successor and the strand ticket. No behavior changes yet — old and new endpoints both answer, both write to (or read from) data the other can see, because Chapter 8's own dependency rule already requires this coexistence to be safe.
2. **Hospital checkpoint.** The moment the successor flag reaches hospital stage (Chapter 8: load-bearing, per its own definition), the legacy endpoint's grace-window clock starts.
3. **De-navigation checkpoint.** After one full additional release cycle beyond the hospital checkpoint (matching Chapter 8's own "minimum 4 weeks" hospital-stage bar, counted again from the checkpoint, not reused from the flag's own soak), the legacy endpoint is removed from every new-client code path — its route registration stays in `routes/index.ts`, its handler stays live, but no shipped client, macro, or internal caller targets it anymore. This is D-27's "de-navigated... until a successor is live plus one release," made concrete.

No endpoint skips checkpoint 1 or 2 to reach checkpoint 3 faster, even under schedule pressure — the checkpoints exist because Chapter 8's own maturity bars are the only evidence this handbook accepts that a successor is safe to make load-bearing for patient data.

**Grace windows and successor mapping.**

| Legacy endpoint | Successor | Trigger flag / condition | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `POST /api/usg-reports/:id/finalize` | `POST /api/radiology/reports/{id}/sign` (Final shape, §10.3) | `ff_radiology_structured_core` **and** `ff_radiology_render_v2` both at hospital stage (sign must trust both the structured findings and the render pipeline that produced the body) | Explicitly named in D-27 as staying live until this migration is explicit — this table is that migration's trigger definition |
| `GET|POST /api/smart-radiology/amendments/:reportDraftId`, `POST /api/usg-reports/:id/amend` | `POST /api/radiology/reports/{id}/amendments` (Final shape) | Same trigger as the finalize row — amendments cannot consolidate onto a sign path that isn't itself consolidated | Both legacy routes deprecate together, not staggered |
| `POST /api/smart-radiology/ai-impressions` | `POST /api/ai-reporting/query` | None — D-30 already decided this; deprecation signal fires immediately, no flag gate | Already frozen for new callers; this table just records the formal deprecation entry |
| Client-side whole-catalog fetch under `radiologyQuickFindings.ts` | Server-side paginated/filtered search endpoint (§10.3) | `ff_radiology_search_v2` at hospital stage | `search_v2` runs its own lighter ladder per Chapter 8 §8.4 — the department/hospital checkpoints still apply, just on a faster clock |
| Legacy whole-catalog client delivery (pre-bundle) | Versioned per-modality delta-sync endpoint | `ff_radiology_catalog_delta` at hospital stage | Also requires the Chapter 1 importer (C-01) to exist, independent of any flag |
| `radiology_structured_templates` legacy routes, other frozen template families | `structured_report_templates` routes | Already frozen per D-29 | Deprecation entry recorded now; no flag trigger needed, mirrors the ai-impressions row |
| Workflow-era `radiologist_macros` endpoints (`radiologyWorkflow.ts`) | `report-generator` `macros`/`smart-macros` endpoints | Already canonical per D-25 | One-time content migration script (D-25) runs once; deprecation entry recorded now |

**Client migration checkpoints, restated per rollout stage** (so an engineer reading Chapter 8 and this chapter together sees one consistent picture):

- **Internal / pilot stages of a successor flag:** legacy endpoint remains the only endpoint new UI targets; the successor is reachable only by engineering/QA or a named pilot cohort per Chapter 8 §8.4 — no deprecation annotation yet.
- **Department stage:** deprecation-annotation checkpoint fires (item 1 above); successor becomes the default for department-wide clients; legacy endpoint keeps answering unchanged for anything not yet cut over.
- **Hospital stage:** grace-window clock starts (item 2); the successor is now load-bearing per Chapter 8's own rollback-hazard language — this chapter's de-navigation clock and Chapter 8's "load-bearing" status start at the same moment by design, so there is exactly one date an engineer needs to remember per flag.
- **De-navigation, one release later:** item 3 fires; legacy route stays registered and functional (D-27) but is removed from every shipped client and internal caller.
- **Multi-center stage:** does not re-trigger any of the above. A new site's flags start their own internal→pilot→department→hospital ladder scoped to that site's `branch_id` (Chapter 8 §8.4's multi-center row), but because the legacy endpoints named in this table are already de-navigated fleet-wide by the time a second site is onboarded, new sites are built directly onto the Final shape and never route new work through a Current-stage endpoint at all.

**What is never deprecated.** `GET/PATCH /api/feature-flags` (Chapter 8's own mechanism), the version/system endpoints (`/api/system/version*`, `/health*`), and the locking endpoint are permanent infrastructure, not lifecycle-flag-gated content surfaces — they are out of this chapter's deprecation machinery entirely.

### Acceptance criteria

- Every endpoint listed in §10.1 is verified present and behaviorally unchanged in production before any Intermediate-stage work begins against it.
- Every Intermediate-stage change lands as a new endpoint or a new optional field; a contract test suite asserts that a client sending only pre-existing required fields to any touched endpoint gets a response satisfying the pre-existing response schema, for every flag combination in Chapter 8's dependency graph.
- No endpoint in the codebase returns an error body outside the `{error, code, details?}` envelope; the status-code taxonomy in §10.3 is enforced by shared middleware, not per-route ad hoc `res.status(...)` literals.
- The sign endpoint (legacy or Final) rejects any request missing `Idempotency-Key` once the convention is switched on for that endpoint, and a verified retry test proves a duplicate `Idempotency-Key` with matching content hash never produces a second `patient_reports` row, a second `report_amendments` row, or a duplicate version-stamping write.
- The catalog importer rejects any import whose declared `catalog_version` is behind the currently-loaded version with a 409, and is a verified no-op (not a re-processing) on a repeat call with an unchanged content hash and version.
- No list endpoint added after this chapter ships uses offset pagination over a table on the "exceeds ~100k rows" list in §10.3; no list endpoint silently accepts and ignores an undocumented filter parameter (C-16's defect class is closed everywhere, not just in `structured-report-templates`).
- Every row in the §10.4 deprecation table has a recorded department-checkpoint annotation in the API inventory before its successor flag is allowed to enter hospital stage per Chapter 8's own gate.
- No legacy route is ever removed from `routes/index.ts` or stops answering — de-navigation is verified by absence from shipped client code and internal callers, never by route deletion.

### Build order

1. Freeze and publish the §10.1 inventory against production routes verbatim — no dependency, do this before any other item in this chapter.
2. Build the shared error-envelope middleware and status-code taxonomy (§10.3) and apply it to all newly written endpoints immediately; backfill onto touched legacy endpoints opportunistically as Chapters 1–7's work touches them. Depends on nothing beyond step 1.
3. Implement `Idempotency-Key` handling on the sign path first — this is the higher patient-safety priority and depends on Chapter 2 (Finding Lifecycle) and Chapter 8's `structured_core`/`render_v2` flags reaching the stage where a Final-shape sign endpoint is trustworthy; until then, apply the same idempotency-key rule to the legacy `POST /api/usg-reports/:id/finalize` endpoint directly, since patient safety does not wait for the resource-model rewrite.
4. Implement the same idempotency convention on the catalog importer, depends on Chapter 1 (Catalog Lifecycle) and C-01's importer existing.
5. Build each Intermediate-stage endpoint (§10.2) in the order Chapter 8 §8.3's tiers unlock its owning flag — this chapter supplies the endpoint shape, Chapter 8 supplies the sequencing, and neither reorders the other.
6. Stand up the Final resource-model endpoints (`reports`, `reports/{id}/findings`, `reports/{id}/amendments`, `catalog/*`) only once their prerequisite flags are at hospital stage; run them through their own internal-stage soak as a new surface before any client is cut over, per Chapter 8's general flag discipline.
7. Execute the §10.4 deprecation table's three checkpoints per legacy endpoint as its successor's flag crosses department and then hospital stage; de-navigate only after the one-release grace window — never earlier, never by deleting the route.

---

## Chapter 11 — Testing strategy

Five test layers protect this platform, each catching a different class of regression that the others structurally cannot: unit tests prove a pure function's output is deterministic; integration tests prove the import pipeline, the API contract, and a real migration against real data all hold together; golden-master tests prove that a fixed structured input renders to the exact same clinical text release after release; clinical regression prove that a *catalog content* change doesn't silently move meaning in reports that already exist; and performance tests prove the platform still meets its per-study budgets under a busy reading day's load. As-built, this platform has strong unit coverage of its pure render logic and essentially nothing above that layer: 378 tests across 30 files, zero of which touch finalize, signed-report immutability, the audit hash-chain, amendments, critical-finding escalation, or Form-F (CTO §4.X, D-39), and none of which run anywhere but a developer's laptop — there is no CI system today (C-19). This chapter is the owning chapter for the five importer validations (B-16) and for the read-loop invariants' status as standing regression targets (B-18); it states the CTO-mandated coverage list in full so it stops being a gap and starts being a checklist. Where this chapter names a numeric threshold that no source document specifies (keystroke latency, workspace-open latency), it says so explicitly and sets the floor itself, the same way C-10 and C-18 set floors elsewhere in this handbook.

### Unit — what must be unit-tested

Four categories of pure logic are load-bearing enough that they must never regress silently, and each maps to an owning content chapter that defines *what* the logic does; this chapter mandates only *that it is tested and how*.

1. **Rendering determinism (owns: Chapter 6 Impression Lifecycle, Chapter 2 Finding Lifecycle mechanics).** `fillTemplate`, `renderAbnormality`, `applyRenderedTransition`, `mergeBlock`/`removeBlock`, `mergeImpression`/`removeImpression` must each have a test asserting: (a) identical structured input always produces byte-identical rendered text; (b) an empty parameter strips its placeholder *and* its grammatical chunk (`"at {level}"`, `"on the {side} side"`, `"measuring {value} mm"`), never leaving a dangling fragment; (c) a manually edited sentence is never overwritten by exact-match removal (the "radiologist's wording wins" guarantee) — assert removal is skipped once the stored text no longer matches byte-for-byte; (d) select-then-deselect round-trips to the pre-select state exactly, for both findings and impression bullets (Bug-001's regression class). Where orchestration logic lives inside a component the test runner cannot import (the root vitest config cannot resolve `@/` aliases, so anything inside `RadiologyReportingWorkspace.tsx` is untestable directly), the mandate is the F1b pattern: extract the logic into an alias-free pure module under `lib/`, and where extraction genuinely cannot happen yet, add a source-text structural regression guard (a regex assertion over the component's source, as `removeImpressionShadowing.test.ts` does) as an interim, never a permanent, substitute for a real unit test.
2. **Parameter resolution (owns: Chapter 3 Parameter Lifecycle).** Severity/laterality/location scale resolution must have a test per ordered set proving append-only behavior is respected (B-04: inserting a new value never changes the index of an existing one) and that `sev.hydro`/`sev.ascites` are the only two scales exposing "gross" (D-07) — a test that any other `sev.*` fixture rejects "gross" as a value guards this structurally, not just by convention.
3. **Recommendation merge and scale↔management binding (owns: Chapter 4 Recommendation Lifecycle).** The B-05 structural guarantee — no seed, insert, or sign path yields a severity category without its bound management line — needs a direct unit test against `sev.birads` (and, when TI-RADS/PI-RADS land, against each future mandatory-management scale): construct every category value in the scale and assert a management line is present and non-empty for all of them. A second test asserts the D-06 dedupe: firing a BI-RADS-5 finding produces exactly one `crit.birads5` trigger, never two, even though both the scale-side criticality map and the registry entry reference the same condition. A third asserts D-16c: `rec.fup_mmg6m`'s stored text equals `sev.birads`'s category-3 management line verbatim — this is the same check the importer runs (see Integration, below), duplicated here as a fast unit-level tripwire.
4. **Impression composition (owns: Chapter 6).** Worst-first assembly (critical > significant > rest, B-14) needs a unit test per ordering tier, plus the hard invariant test: a normal-study impression fragment can never coexist with any positive finding in the same assembled impression — assert this by construction (build an impression with one positive fragment and confirm the normal-study fragment is excluded), not by hoping the block rule in the pack is never bypassed.
5. **Measurement auto-calc formulas (owns: Chapter 5 Measurement Lifecycle).** Every pinned formula (D-10: MLS critical ≥5 mm, CTR >0.50 PA, prostate volume L×W×H×0.52, CTSS /25, reflux >0.5 s superficial / >1.0 s deep, ABI bands 0.9/0.5, Evans >0.30) needs a table-driven unit test with boundary-value cases (exactly at the threshold, one unit below, one unit above) — boundary values are exactly where a critical trigger like MLS ≥5 mm silently drifts if a formula is ever refactored. A companion test enforces D-08: the canal-stenosis grading path must never assert a numeric-derived severity — assert that no code path maps a raw millimeter measurement directly into `sev.canal`'s category without an explicit radiologist gesture; a raw AP-diameter value may only ever appear as a *suggested* grade, offer-never-act.

**Confidence-band and provisional-marker unit coverage (owns: Chapter 7 AI Interaction Lifecycle, tested here).** Two structural guarantees from D-22 and C-12 are cheap to unit-test and expensive to get wrong: (a) the band-mapping function must never emit a raw percentage, a color other than grey/amber, or a High/Medium/Low label at the reading surface — test every internal score tier maps to exactly one of *Routine / Worth a look / Attention*, with low-tier extraction explicitly landing on *Attention*; (b) the provisional-marker-clearing function must return "still provisional" for every input that is not an edit or an explicit section confirm — a test that feeds it a large dwell/viewport-time value and asserts the marker does **not** clear is the direct regression guard against re-introducing dwell-based clearing.

**Where unit tests live and run.** Co-located `*.test.ts` beside the source file, vitest, invoked via `pnpm test`. Pure logic belongs in alias-free `lib/` modules specifically so it is reachable by the root vitest config — this is a hard authoring rule, not a style preference, because it is the only thing currently making F1a/F1b's logic testable at all.

### Integration — import pipeline, API contract, migration-against-restore

**Import pipeline round-trips (owning content: Chapter 1 Catalog Lifecycle; this chapter owns the validation contract itself, B-16).** The catalog importer (`scripts/src/import-radiology-catalog.ts`, C-01) must run, in CI dry-run mode on every PR touching `seeds/radiology/content-packs/` and again at real import, exactly these five fail-loud validations — none of the five may degrade to a warning:

1. **Reference integrity** — every `param./sev./lat./loc./meas./rec./crit./tpl.` reference inside a finding block resolves against the loaded shared libraries after the two-pass forward-reference resolution (D-14: pass 1 loads all keys across the full load order — `_shared_libraries.yaml` → `_v1_1` → `_v1_2` → `_screening_whole_spine.yaml` → study packs — pass 2 validates references).
2. **Alias rules** — 2–6 lowercase characters (D-11), unique within a study, no alias a strict prefix of another in the same study *after* level-stem expansion (never validate the stem itself, C-05), reserved globals `nad`/`urgent`/`clincorr` never shadowed.
3. **`{slot}` integrity** — every `{slot}` marker in a sentence template resolves to a parameter, measurement, or derived value the finding actually binds.
4. **Management-line binding for scales** — every mandatory-management scale (B-05) has a non-empty management line for every category value it defines; this is the import-time twin of the unit test in the Unit section above.
5. **`id_key` immutability** — a duplicate or renamed `id_key` is always a hard error; the importer never resolves an `id_key` collision by bumping a suffix the way the legacy billing importer does (B-01, D-16 conflates none of this — keys are API).

Round-trip fixtures needed: one **positive** fixture pack per validation (imports cleanly) and one **negative** fixture pack per validation (deliberately violates exactly that rule) so each of the five has an explicit "this must fail" test, not just an absence of failures on the real packs. A sixth integration test asserts idempotency: importing the same pack version twice produces zero net row change on the second run (mirrors the billing importer's proven idempotency pattern, applied to the new importer). A seventh asserts the D-16 conversion-bug checks specifically (no inlined value list where a key reference is required; no nested-format `recommendation` display array coexisting with `recommendation_code`; `derived_from` textual equality, D-16c) against the shipped packs — these three are known, already-committed violations that must be fixed and then guarded, not aspirational.

**API contract tests, including the six CTO-flagged report-lifecycle paths (D-39).** These are named here in full because their absence is the single largest coverage gap in the codebase today; each needs a positive and a negative test, hitting the real Express routes against a test database, not a mock:

| Path | What the test must assert |
|---|---|
| **Finalize** | Authorship is server-derived from the authenticated session (`subjectName`); a request with a client-supplied author string is ignored, not trusted (D-33). `canFinalize`/`canApprove` are enforced on the route, not merely defined — a `radiology_typist` role attempting finalize is rejected. The sign gate blocks exactly the D-20/master-spec §13.2 set (✕ contradictions, unmet mandatory fields, untouched provisional spans, unstarted escalations for a written critical finding) and clears once each is resolved — test each blocking condition individually, both present and resolved. |
| **Signed-report immutability** | Once a report is SIGNED, no route can mutate `patient_reports.body` — attempt a direct update and assert rejection. Post-finalize writes to the legacy projections (`radiology_studies.final_report`, `teleradiology_assignments.final_report`) are rejected at the route level (D-19). Content hash recorded at finalize is re-computable and matches on re-verification. |
| **Audit hash-chain** | Two concurrent audit writes never produce a hash collision or lost update — a test that fires simultaneous writes and asserts the advisory-lock serialization holds (D-34); the unique index on chain hash rejects a duplicate; a raw `UPDATE`/`DELETE` against an audit row is rejected (by `REVOKE` or trigger, whichever lands); the verification endpoint recomputes the chain and reports a match against a known-good fixture and a mismatch against a tampered one. |
| **Amendments** | Once SIGNED, all content changes go through `report_amendments` and only through it — no route reachable from the client can otherwise touch a signed body (D-19). An amendment chain of length >1 preserves every prior amendment, not just the latest. Amendment routes enforce the same RBAC as finalize (D-33). |
| **Critical-finding escalation** | Firing a `crit.*` entry pre-fills `rec.urgent`; the sign gate blocks while escalation is unstarted for a written critical finding (midline shift ≥5 mm and the other hard measurement-triggered criticals get a dedicated test each); the communication timestamp auto-documents on completion; median written→communicated time is emitted as a measurable value the scorecard chapter can read (B-07). A separate test asserts C-04's determinism rule directly: with the AI/copilot layer mocked to return nothing, every hard critical trigger (midline shift ≥5 mm, BI-RADS 5) and every ✕-blocking rule still fires — proving no blocking or critical gate is silently dependent on model availability. |
| **Form-F** | For any fetal/obstetric USG study, finalize must be rejected while its linked `form_f_records` row is missing or incomplete (statutory PC-PNDT fields: ID-card verification, consent date, doctor name, centre registration at minimum), and must succeed once complete. As-built today there is no evidence this gate exists in the finalize path at all (`artifacts/api-server/src/routes/form-f.ts` and `usgReports.ts` reference the table but no finalize-time completeness check was found) — building the gate itself is out of this chapter's scope (Chapter 12, Security/compliance, owns the rule), but this chapter mandates that CI must not claim "Form-F coverage" via a vacuous test once the gate exists: the test must exercise a genuinely incomplete record and a genuinely complete one. |

Beyond the six, extend the existing as-built contract-test precedent (`featureFlags.test.ts`, `radiology-report-generator.test.ts`) rather than replacing it: the A3.2 dual-write guard (`findings !== undefined`, not `.length > 0`; `findings: []` clears rows, an absent key leaves them untouched) and the A5 drift-check read-only guarantee already have coverage and must keep it as `report_finding_instances` grows new writers.

**DB migration tests against production-restore snapshots.** Per D-35 (on-prem `pg_dump` to `/volume1/backup/` is the backup source of truth) and D-36 (the two sanctioned migration paths, fail-closed under `ON_ERROR_STOP=1`), a scheduled test stage — not a per-PR CI gate, because it needs a full restore — does the following against a scratch Postgres instance on every release candidate:

1. Restore the latest verified backup.
2. Run both sanctioned paths in order: Drizzle migrations via `care-migrate`'s `push-ci` mechanics, then the registered Step-5 feature migrations (never `drizzle-kit push`, never `push --force`).
3. Assert the migration run exits 0 under `ON_ERROR_STOP=1` — a partial-apply-then-continue outcome is a hard test failure, not a warning, closing exactly the gap D-36 flags as HIGH.
4. Assert row counts on `patient_reports`, `report_finding_instances`, and every audit table are unchanged pre/post migration (a migration that silently drops or truncates clinical rows must fail this test, not ship).
5. Assert `zz_schema_reconcile`'s warning-downgrade path is only ever exercised for pre-existing legacy drift on the restored snapshot, never for a newly-introduced migration's own failure — if a new migration's failure gets masked by the reconcile file's exception handler, that is itself a bug this test must catch.

This is the only test layer in this chapter that requires a full database restore; it therefore runs on a release cadence (Chapter 13, Deployment, owns exactly when), not on every commit.

### Golden master — the golden-report corpus

**What a golden is.** For each of the ten v1 study types (`mri_brain`, `mri_ls_spine`, `mri_c_spine`, `ct_brain`, `usg_abdomen`, `usg_kub`, `hrct_chest`, `cxr`, `doppler_ll`, `mammography`), a golden case is a pinned pair: (a) a canonical **structured input** — a fixed set of finding instances with their parameters, severities, measurements, and laterality, expressed exactly as `report_finding_instances`/`AbnormalityInstance` rows would hold them; (b) the **exact expected rendered text** the render engine (`renderAbnormality` → `applyRenderedTransition` → the four sections: findings, impression, technique, recommendation) produces from that input, at a pinned `RENDER_ENGINE_VERSION` and `catalog_version` pair. A golden test is a pure equality assertion: current render output for the pinned input must equal the stored golden text.

**Coverage requirement, per study type, minimum four cases:**
1. The study's highest-frequency tile or combo (e.g. USG Whole Abdomen's `#1` tile, "FL Gr I, rest NAD").
2. At least one critical-registry-triggering combination, for every study that has a `crit.*` entry (nine of the ten v1 studies; MRI LS Spine has none per the master spec's study table) — proving `rec.urgent` fills correctly end to end.
3. The study's normal template (`tpl.normal.<study>[_<variant>]`) rendered alone — including both `doppler_ll` variants (D-17).
4. A scale↔management binding case for every study carrying a mandatory-management scale — mammography's BI-RADS category + ACR composition + dense-breast rider (B-05) is the sharpest instance and must be one of the four.

**Storage.** Goldens are checked into git as plain-text/JSON fixture files co-located with the render-engine tests (not a binary blob, not a database table) — git-diffable and reviewable in a normal PR diff, the same way the shared-library packs are. One file per study type keeps a PR's diff scoped to the study it actually touched.

**Versioning.** Every golden file is stamped with the `render_engine_version` and `catalog_version` pair it was blessed under (independent namespaces per B-30/D-18 — neither derives from the other, and neither derives from the app's own `version.json`). A golden test only asserts equality when both stamps match the code under test's current values; if either has moved since the golden was last blessed, the test must fail loudly and explicitly name which version moved — never silently skip and never silently pass a stale comparison.

**Blessing process.** Any catalog version bump (MINOR/PATCH per B-03) or render-engine version bump that changes rendered output triggers regeneration of every golden the change could plausibly touch. A human — the content editor plus one clinical reviewer, mirroring the Chapter 1 review-gate pattern — reviews the line-by-line diff between the old golden text and the freshly rendered text before the new golden file is committed. The rule for what counts as an acceptable diff: a PATCH-level (wording-only) catalog bump's golden diff must contain *only* the intended wording change; any diff a reviewer cannot immediately attribute to the PR's stated intent — a slot rendering wrong, a placeholder chunk not stripping, an unrelated study's text moving — is a blocking failure of that PR, not something to wave through because "the tests still pass." Committing the regenerated golden file alongside the version stamps happens in the same PR as the catalog/engine change; a golden file is never updated in a separate, unreviewed commit.

**Diff review mechanics in CI.** Golden tests run on every PR (C-19); a failing golden blocks merge. The PR template (Chapter 1's review gate) must carry a line stating which goldens the PR intentionally changed and why; a reviewer approving the PR is attesting they read that diff, not that "CI is green."

**As-built gap.** No golden corpus exists today. `renderEngine.test.ts` uses ad hoc inline fixtures scoped to proving F1a/F1b's re-export and reducer behavior — a real behavioral proof, but not a blessed, study-keyed corpus, and it carries no version stamp. This entire section is net-new work, and it is the single highest-priority net-new item in this chapter: it is the only guardrail catalog content changes will have until it exists.

### Clinical regression — catalog content changes gated by re-render + shadow replay

Every catalog content change that reaches MINOR or higher (B-03) — and every PATCH-level wording change too, since wording changes are exactly what golden diffs are built to catch — must clear two gates before merge, in this order:

**Gate 1 — full golden re-render.** The entire golden corpus (above) re-renders under the proposed catalog version; any diff outside what the PR declares as intentional blocks the merge. This alone catches structural regressions (a slot breaking, a template drifting) but cannot catch drift specific to real, messier clinical content the fixed golden inputs don't happen to exercise.

**Gate 2 — sampled real signed reports, replayed in shadow mode.** For every catalog entry the PR touches that has ever been used by a signed report, pull a sample of already-signed `patient_reports` rows — minimum 25 per affected study type, or every signed report touching that entry in the trailing 90 days, whichever is larger — and, using their stamped `structured_json`/`catalog_version`/`template_version` (frozen at sign per D-18/C-02), re-run them through the current render engine plus the proposed catalog version in a sandbox path that **never writes**: never touches `patient_reports`, never re-triggers `crit.*` escalation side effects (a shadow-mode critical trigger must be observed and logged, never dispatched to WhatsApp/SMS/the real escalation workflow), and runs against access-controlled report content per the PHI handling rules Chapter 12 (Security) owns. The output is a diff between the original signed text and the newly re-rendered text for every sampled report. A catalog PR touching only draft-status entries or entries no signed report has ever used skips Gate 2 — there is nothing to regress against — but still clears Gate 1.

**Sign-off workflow for shadow-mode diffs.** The clinical owner triages every non-empty diff into exactly one of three buckets:

1. **Intended wording change, already approved.** The diff matches what Gate 1's golden-diff review already approved for this PR — no separate action needed.
2. **Unintended but clinically inert** (whitespace, punctuation, a synonym substitution with no clinical weight). Requires clinical-owner sign-off with a one-line logged reason. Merge may proceed once logged.
3. **Clinically material and unintended.** Hard block. The catalog PR cannot merge until the cause is found and fixed, full stop — this bucket exists specifically to catch the class of error a fixed golden corpus, by construction, cannot: real clinical text combinations the corpus doesn't happen to cover.

A MAJOR catalog bump (key removal or semantic change, B-03) requires both the clinical owner and the Chief Clinical Architect (or delegate) to sign the shadow-mode diff report specifically, in addition to the golden-diff review — a MAJOR bump can move key semantics a golden corpus gap might not surface, so the shadow-mode report gets the same double sign-off Chapter 1 requires for MAJOR bumps generally.

**As-built gap.** None of this exists: no catalog importer to version-bump against (Chapter 1, C-01), no provenance linkage from a catalog `id_key` to the signed reports that used it (Chapter 7's audit trail, which this depends on), and no shadow-replay harness. This section's build order therefore sits strictly behind Chapter 1's importer and Chapter 7's per-suggestion/per-finding audit trail landing — see Build order, below.

### Performance — latency budgets, load profile, CI enforcement

**The five per-study UX budgets are the top-level acceptance criteria** (master spec §3.1, cited fully so this chapter is self-contained): clicks to signed report ≤8 (0 on the green path, down from a 25–40 baseline); 100% keyboard-only completion of the reporting loop; ≤6 long saccades (report ↔ far panel/tab, down from 15–25); ≤1 interruption per study and never during dictation (down from 3–6); 8–10 minutes time-to-signed-report for a routine study (down from 18–20). These are validated primarily by the live-session acceptance test in the UX review (2–3 staff radiologists, stopwatch and click counter) at release-one — that human validation is out of this chapter's scope to automate away, but the four latency/throughput budgets below are this chapter's automatable proxies for the same targets, enforced continuously rather than once per release.

**Keystroke-to-render.** The source doctrine is absolute but not numeric: "protect the input loop above all other rendering work, forever" (P1) and "zero non-critical interruptions during dictation" is a standing bug class. No source document gives a millisecond figure. This chapter sets the enforceable floor: P95 keystroke-to-character-visible latency must stay under 50 ms on the reference reading-workstation hardware profile, measured with a synthetic input-latency harness (a scripted keystroke-timing test, e.g. via Playwright) that runs *with* the lint gutter, the AI margin-card panel, and autosave all active and mid-cycle — the harness exists specifically to catch the regression class the debounced-quality-checks posture is meant to prevent, so it must never be run against an idle page. A failing run is a P0 regression, not a backlog item, precisely because P1 has no fallback tier.

**Report-open.** No source document gives a number for Reporting Workspace time-to-interactive (worklist claim → Orient stage rendered with prior-report one-liner, safety flags, and protocol QA verdict visible, editor focused and typeable). This chapter sets the floor: P95 under 1.5 s on LAN to the on-prem PACS/DB, explicitly **excluding** DICOM image streaming to the viewer iframe, which is a separate budget this handbook does not own (imaging infrastructure, not the reporting workspace). A performance test asserts the 1.5 s figure against the reporting surface only, with the viewer route pre-staged but not yet painted, matching the "viewer route known by first study" pre-stage contract.

**Sign.** This is a direct source number, not one this chapter invents: green-path sign (`Ctrl+Enter`, no unresolved critical lint) must serve the next pre-staged study in under 1 second, with the PDF/PACS-archive/referrer-email/patient-SMS pipeline running entirely in the background (master spec §3.3/§9.4, B-19). A performance test asserts the <1 s figure measures only "next study interactive," never blocking on any leg of the background pipeline; a second, non-blocking assertion tracks background-pipeline completion time as a monitored SLA (median under 30 seconds, alert past 5 minutes) rather than a hard gate, since a slow PACS archive write must never be allowed to slow down the reading loop it happens behind.

**Load profile of a busy reading day.** The master spec's own load figure is the input: a radiologist signs 60–120 studies/day. A load test must simulate N concurrent radiologist sessions, each independently performing the full loop (worklist claim → tile toggles → autosave every few seconds → periodic AI-panel calls → sign) at a rate consistent with the low end of that range sustained across a shift, ramped to a peak concurrency matching the practice's actual radiologist headcount reading simultaneously. Because the current deployment is a single-container API with an in-process 30-second-TTL feature-flag cache (as-built, no horizontal scaling) and an explicit `pg` pool ceiling of `max: 20` (C-18), the load test's job is specifically to find where that single-box ceiling bites: pool exhaustion under peak concurrent autosave, feature-flag cache staleness windows under rapid PATCH+read cycles, and leading-wildcard `ILIKE` search cost (C-18's known bounded cost, pending `pg_trgm` under `ff_radiology_search_v2`) under realistic query volume. A load test that never exceeds the pool ceiling has not tested the busy-day scenario; it must be run past it to find the actual breaking point, even though production is expected to stay under it today.

**Read-loop invariants as standing regression targets (B-18; invariants owned by Chapter 7).** These are not latency numbers, they are behavioral guarantees, and they belong in the performance/regression suite because a violation of any one of them is exactly the kind of thing that only shows up under real interaction load, not in a unit test:

- The report canvas never moves, resizes, or scrolls as a side effect of any panel activity — a regression test drives panel updates (AI card arriving, lint mark appearing, worklist refresh) while asserting canvas scroll position and layout bounds are unchanged.
- No background completion, refetch, or panel update ever steals editor focus — a test asserts focus remains on the editor across a burst of simulated background events.
- Nothing non-critical fires during dictation or within 5 seconds of a keystroke — a test drives continuous typing/simulated dictation and asserts zero non-critical notification-channel events fire during the window, only ledger writes (silent) are permitted.
- Re-authentication, network-profile changes, and app updates never interrupt an open, unsigned report — a test forces each of these three conditions mid-draft and asserts the draft remains open, editable, and unsaved-work-safe.
- Draft state survives crash, close, and roaming between workstations — a test kills the client process mid-edit and asserts the draft reloads with no data loss on the next open, on the same or a different workstation.

**Where performance tests run in CI.** The keystroke-to-render harness and the golden-master suite run on every PR (C-19's pipeline: `pnpm test`, `pnpm typecheck`, lint, pack validations, plus this harness). The report-open and sign latency assertions run on every PR against a seeded test dataset sized to be representative, not against production data. The full busy-reading-day load simulation and the migration-against-restore test (Integration, above) are both too expensive to run per-PR; they run on a release-candidate cadence gating the release train (Chapter 13, Deployment), not blocking individual merges.

### Gap summary (as-built vs. mandated)

| Layer | As-built today | Net-new work this chapter mandates |
|---|---|---|
