| Unit | Strong: render engine, quick-findings merge, feature-flag lib/routes, structured-json cache/drift, schema-typing tests (202-line as-built-code digest inventory) | Scale↔management binding test, D-06 dedupe test, canal-severity non-assertion test, confidence-band mapping test, provisional-marker dwell-immunity test |
| Integration | None for the catalog importer (doesn't exist yet); partial for save-draft dual-write and drift endpoint | Full B-16 five-validation suite with positive/negative fixtures; all six D-39 report-lifecycle contract-test paths; migration-against-restore stage |
| Golden master | None — inline ad hoc fixtures only | Full ten-study, four-case-minimum blessed corpus with version stamping and blessing workflow |
| Clinical regression | None | Shadow-mode replay harness, sampling policy, three-bucket sign-off workflow |
| Performance | None automated; only the planned live-session stopwatch acceptance test | Keystroke-latency harness, report-open harness, sign-latency assertion, busy-day load simulation, five read-loop-invariant regression tests |
| CI | None — `pnpm test`/`pnpm typecheck` are developer-local only | GitHub Actions pipeline per C-19 running unit + integration + golden on every PR; migration-restore and load tests on release cadence |

### Acceptance criteria

- A CI pipeline (C-19) runs `pnpm test`, `pnpm typecheck` against the checked-in `typecheck-baseline.txt` ceiling, lint, and the importer dry-run validations on every PR, and a PR cannot merge with any of them red.
- Every one of the five B-16 importer validations has both a passing (positive) and a failing (negative) fixture pack exercised in CI, not just clean runs against the real content packs.
- All six D-39 report-lifecycle paths (finalize, signed-report immutability, audit hash-chain, amendments, critical-finding escalation, Form-F) have contract tests with at least one positive and one negative case each; a `grep` for `finalReport|immutab|hash-chain|critical-find|amend|form.?f` across the test tree returns non-zero matches for every term.
- A golden-master corpus exists for all ten v1 study types with at least four blessed cases each, version-stamped with `render_engine_version` + `catalog_version`, and runs on every PR.
- The clinical-regression shadow-mode harness can, for any catalog PR touching an already-used entry, produce a diff report against a real sample of signed reports without writing to `patient_reports` or firing a real escalation notification.
- The keystroke-to-render synthetic harness runs with the lint gutter, AI panel, and autosave all active, and fails the build on any P95 result at or above 50 ms.
- A load test has been run past the current `pg` pool ceiling (`max: 20`, C-18) at least once, with its breaking point documented, before this platform is represented as validated for a full busy reading day.
- The five read-loop invariants (B-18) each have a passing automated regression test, independent of the live-session stopwatch acceptance exercise.
- The migration-against-restore test has run successfully against the most recent verified backup before any release is promoted (Chapter 13 gate).

### Build order

1. Stand up the CI pipeline itself (C-19) first — nothing else in this chapter has a home to run in otherwise. This has no dependency on any other chapter.
2. Land the unit-test gap items (scale↔management binding, D-06 dedupe, canal-severity non-assertion, confidence-band mapping, provisional-marker dwell-immunity) in parallel with CI stand-up — they depend only on the logic Chapters 3–7 already define, most of which is already implemented.
3. Build the golden-master corpus next, before the catalog importer lands — the golden cases can and should be authored against the *current* render engine and the shipped YAML packs' content even while the importer doesn't exist yet, since the render engine already consumes equivalent structured input today. This is the single highest-priority net-new item; do not defer it behind the importer.
4. Land Chapter 1's catalog importer (C-01), then immediately wire its five B-16 validations into the CI dry-run gate with positive/negative fixtures — this chapter's Integration section assumes the importer exists; until it does, only the fixture-authoring work can proceed.
5. Build the six D-39 API contract-test suites against the current finalize/amend routes as they exist today (do not wait for any redesign) — these are testing what's *supposed* to already be true (D-19/D-20/D-33), and several will fail immediately against as-built code (notably Form-F, which appears to have no finalize-time gate at all); a failing test that documents a real gap is more valuable here than a green suite that hides one.
6. Build the migration-against-restore test stage once Chapter 13's backup/restore pipeline (D-35) is confirmed reliable — this test depends on having a trustworthy restore to run against.
7. Build the clinical-regression shadow-mode harness last among the net-new items: it depends on the golden corpus (step 3) for Gate 1, the catalog importer (step 4) for real version bumps to test against, and Chapter 7's per-suggestion/per-finding audit/provenance trail for the id_key-to-signed-report linkage that picks the shadow sample.
8. Build the performance harnesses (keystroke-to-render, report-open, sign-latency) in parallel with steps 2–3; they depend only on the Reporting Workspace existing, not on any other testing-chapter work. Schedule the full busy-day load simulation for after the single-container deployment (Chapter 13) is finalized, since its ceiling (`pg` pool `max: 20`) is what the load test is validated against.

---

## Chapter 12 — Deployment strategy

This chapter maps the five-stage flag ladder Chapter 8 defines in the abstract (internal → pilot → department → hospital → multi-center) onto the concrete, single-box Synology reality this platform actually runs on: one NAS, one Postgres, one Docker Compose stack, no separate staging hardware. Chapter 8 owns the flag-by-flag mechanics — the dependency graph, the per-flag entry/exit table, the one-way-door and rollback-effect tables; this chapter owns the physical and organizational execution layered on top of it: what "staging" means when there is no second box, how a shadow-mode comparison and a rollback drill are actually run, in what order the ten v1 modalities get cut over to the Reporting Workspace, what "hospital-wide" integration load and downtime look like on this hardware, and how a second physical site gets onboarded without redesigning the schema. It is the owning chapter for B-28 (never hardcode infrastructure) and states that rule fully here. It relies on Chapter 8 for flag mechanics, Chapter 9 for migration and backup mechanics (D-35, D-36), and Chapter 14 for the metrics dashboards it cites as monitoring signals — none of that is re-derived below.

### 12.1 Deployment topology and pipeline grounding

**There is one environment, not three.** The as-built system has no separate dev, staging, and production hosts — `docker-compose.yml` defines one stack (`care-db` → `care-migrate` → `care-schema-verify`/`care-db-patch-v2` → `care-api` → `care-web`, container-ordered via `service_completed_successfully`/`service_healthy` conditions) deployed to one Synology NAS at `/volume1/docker/care-diagnostics`. "Dev/staging on the Synology environment" in this chapter's mandate does not mean a second NAS; it means a **logically isolated dataset on the same physical stack**, because Master Spec §17's Should/Future tiers and the CTO review's cost estimates both assume a single-clinic budget. Do not provision a second NAS for Internal-stage work; it is not needed and it re-introduces the multi-environment schema-drift problem Chapter 9 already fixed for restores (`zz_schema_reconcile`).

**Deploy mechanics, restated only to the extent this chapter depends on them.** `deploy-synology.sh` runs `bump-build.cjs` (monotonic build number, forward-only even on rollback, per B-30), builds via `docker compose build --no-cache`, then `up -d`; this is a **3–5 minute full-stack outage** on every deploy — there is no blue/green or expand/contract path today, so "deploy" and "release" are the same event for anything not gated by a `feature_flags` row. Every stage below therefore distinguishes **flag flips** (instant, no outage, reversible per Chapter 8 §8.5) from **code deploys** (an outage, forward-only, git-revert-and-rebuild to roll back). A stage's entry criteria that says "code-complete" always implies at least one deploy already happened before that stage's clock starts.

**Synthetic-data marking (decided here — no source document specifies a mechanism).** Internal-stage testing needs real rows moving through the real pipeline (Quick Select, lint, render engine, escalation workflow) without those rows ever being mistaken for a real patient in analytics, billing, or a referring-clinician-facing output. Add a nullable `is_synthetic boolean` column, default null (additive-only per B-23), to **`dicom_studies`** (canonical PACS/DICOM identity, D-28) and to **`radiology_worklist`** (the operational worklist key queried directly by dashboards) — both via one of Chapter 9's sanctioned feature-migration paths (D-36 path 2), never `runStartupMigrations()`. Do **not** add a column to `patients` (🟢 shared/core, touches billing) — synthetic patient identities are instead created with a reserved naming convention (surname prefix `ZZTEST-`, a fixed out-of-range MRN block) documented in the runbook, which is sufficient to keep them out of billing and referring-clinician views without touching a protected shared table. Every query that feeds Chapter 14's dashboards, the turnaround-time scorecard, or any referring-clinician-facing output MUST filter `is_synthetic IS NOT TRUE`; this filter is a required-review item on every new analytics query, not an opt-in.

**Monitoring surfaces used throughout this chapter.** `GET /api/system/version` (app version, build number, `schema_deploy_state`) and `GET /api/health/schema` (Drizzle vs. feature migration counts) are the two endpoints every stage's "deploy succeeded" check reads before anything else proceeds; a deploy that doesn't show its expected build number and a clean migration count on both endpoints is not complete, regardless of what `docker compose ps` reports.

**How this chapter's stages relate to Chapter 8's ladder.** Chapter 8 §8.4 defines the five stage names generically and reuses them for every flag independently — "Pilot" for `structured_core` and "Pilot" for `ai_assist` are different calendar events, months apart, each satisfying the same generic entry/exit template. This chapter uses the identical five names for a different, coarser thing: the **program-wide** posture of the whole Reporting Workspace rollout, which modality waves are live, who is instructed to touch the system, and what physical/organizational machinery (backups, downtime SOP, training) must exist before real patients are exposed to any of it. A flag can be at its own Pilot stage (Chapter 8) while the program as a whole is already at Department stage (this chapter) for other, earlier-landed flags — the two are read together, not collapsed into one timeline. Where this chapter's tables say "Chapter-8 flags in motion," that is the explicit join between the two.

**Backup and DR grounding for every rollback in this chapter.** Every rollback procedure below that touches real patient data cites "a same-day backup per Chapter 9/D-35" — this means the streaming `pg_dump` (plus WAL archiving) pipeline to `/volume1/backup/` that Chapter 9 owns, restore-verified, never the retired Replit-pull script and never the row-capped in-app path that D-35 fixed for false-success reporting. This chapter does not re-specify the backup mechanism; it specifies **when**, in the rollout, a fresh backup is a hard prerequisite rather than a background schedule item: before any Department-stage or later rollback (§12.4–§12.7), before the partitioning cutover Chapter 9 describes if it happens to coincide with a rollout stage, and before every site-onboarding cutover (§12.7). A rollback attempted without a same-day backup on hand is a process violation regardless of whether the rollback itself succeeds.

### 12.2 Infrastructure configuration doctrine (B-28, owning)

**Never hardcode infrastructure.** LAN IPs, viewer URLs (Orthanc/OHIF/Weasis), PACS/Orthanc connection settings, and credentials live **only** in environment variables (infrastructure-level config, never a feature gate — Chapter 8 §8.1 rule 7) or in the admin-editable `pacs_settings`/`clinic_settings` tables. Nothing in application code may contain a literal LAN IP, hostname, or credential.

The tree currently violates this rule in three known places: `viewerService.ts:66-69,495-496` hardcodes `172.16.1.139`, `192.168.1.137`, and `caredeoghar.com` as fallback values; `lib/db/drizzle/0004_seed_pacs_viewer_defaults.sql` seeds the same local IPs as row data. These are **scheduled fixes, not precedents** — no new code may cite them as justification for a fourth hardcoded address. The remediation, in order: (1) confirm every one of these values already has a corresponding admin-configurable key in `pacs_settings`/`clinic_settings` (add one via an additive migration if not); (2) backfill that key from the current hardcoded value in the same migration, so production behavior is unchanged the moment the code path flips; (3) delete the hardcoded fallback from `viewerService.ts`, replacing it with a fail-loud error ("PACS viewer URL not configured") rather than a silent fallback to a guessed address — a missing config value must never resolve to *some* IP, because that IP might be wrong for the box it's running on, especially once a second site exists (§12.7). This remediation is a prerequisite for Hospital stage (§12.5) because integration load at that stage is exactly the scenario where a stale hardcoded fallback silently routes traffic to the wrong PACS node.

`pacs_settings` today is one global key/value table (no `branch_id`); until §12.7's federation model is live, that is correct for a single site and is not a defect to fix early.

### 12.3 Stage — Internal

Engineering and QA only, against synthetic studies, on the single production Synology stack (§12.1) — never a second box, never real patient data.

| | |
|---|---|
| **Entry criteria** | Per Chapter 8 §8.4's Internal row for the specific flag(s) in motion: strand ticket code-complete, CI green including the five importer validations where catalog data is touched (B-16), typecheck baseline unchanged (C-19), zero new A5 drift after a full write/read cycle for any flag writing `report_finding_instances`. Additionally, deployment-specific: `is_synthetic` marking (§12.1) is live and enforced in every dashboard query the flag's feature touches; a minimum of 50 synthetic studies spanning all ten v1 modality prefixes (B-29) are seeded and pass the five importer validations. |
| **Exit criteria** | Chapter 8's 3-business-day flag-on soak with zero unhandled errors attributable to the flagged path, **plus**: for any flag with a text- or grade-producing surface (`classification` D, `render_v2` F, `voice_structured` K, `ai_assist` M), a completed shadow-mode run (below) with a divergence rate low enough to explain every remaining diff by inspection, not by volume. |
| **Monitoring signals** | `/api/system/version` and `/api/health/schema` clean after each deploy (§12.1); CI pipeline (C-19) green on every PR touching the flag's surface; A5 drift-diagnostic endpoint (Chapter 9) returns zero drift on synthetic drafts; shadow-mode divergence log (below) reviewed daily. |
| **Rollback procedure** | PATCH `/api/feature-flags/:key` to disable the flag (Chapter 8 §8.5) — at Internal stage this affects only synthetic data, so no drained-draft concern applies; no backup is required before an Internal-stage rollback because no real patient data is at risk. |
| **Chapter-8 flags in motion** | Per Chapter 8's build order: `structured_core` (A) is the only flag permitted to be at Internal stage alone at program start. Once `structured_core` clears Department (§12.5), Tier 1 flags (`measurement_pool`, `modality_expand`, `hierarchy`, `presets`, `voice_structured`, `search_v2`) begin their own Internal stage in parallel; `classification` (D) and `catalog_delta` (H) follow once `modality_expand` clears Department; `render_v2` (F) and `ai_assist` (M) begin Internal last, per their Tier 3/4 gating. |

**Shadow mode, defined here (answers the mandate's "shadow-mode comparisons" for the program as a whole, not only Pilot).** For any flag whose surface changes what text or grade a radiologist would read (`classification`, `render_v2`, `voice_structured`, `ai_assist`), the Internal-stage build MUST include a shadow-logging sub-mode: with the flag enabled, the new code path executes and its output is diffed against the legacy code path's output for the same input, on every synthetic draft save — but the new output is **never rendered** to a user in this sub-mode. Diffs are written to a dedicated log (table or structured log line, engineer's choice, but must be queryable by flag key and by draft) with a timestamp and a similarity score. A flag may not leave Internal stage until this shadow log shows a divergence rate the engineering owner can explain finding-by-finding — not a percentage threshold picked in advance (there is no clinically-validated number for this), but a reviewed, signed-off log. This shadow-logging sub-mode is retired once the flag reaches Department stage; it is a build-time safety net, not a permanent feature.

### 12.4 Stage — Pilot

One or two named radiologists, briefed that they alone are to exercise the new surface even though — per Chapter 8 §8.4's note on the flag mechanism having no scope column — it is globally live the instant it is PATCHed on.

| | |
|---|---|
| **Entry criteria** | Chapter 8 §8.4 Pilot row: Internal exit criteria met; the kill-switch drill (below) rehearsed and passed before go-live; the pilot radiologist(s) and the coordinator briefed on what "off" looks like. Deployment-specific addition: shadow mode (§12.3) has been running against **real, non-synthetic** draft traffic in log-only mode for at least 2 reading days immediately before the flag is allowed to render live output to the pilot radiologist — this is the first point at which shadow-mode comparison runs against genuine clinical drafts rather than seeded studies, and it is a hard prerequisite, not a nice-to-have. |
| **Exit criteria** | Chapter 8 §8.4 Pilot row: minimum 5 reading days with the pilot radiologist(s); zero ✕-blocking lint regressions; zero missed critical-finding escalations (B-07 unaffected); signed-report immutability spot-checked (D-19) and clean. Deployment-specific: the daily feedback loop (below) produced no unresolved "surprise" item older than 24 hours at exit. |
| **Monitoring signals** | Shadow-mode divergence log (now against real drafts); lint gutter-mark rates (✕/△/◌) for the pilot radiologist compared to their pre-flag baseline; A5 drift-diagnostic on every draft the pilot radiologist touches; the escalation-communication scorecard (median written→communicated <5 min, B-07) unaffected. |
| **Rollback procedure** | The kill-switch drill, run for real if triggered: (1) confirm a same-day `pg_dump` exists per Chapter 9/D-35's backup pipeline — if the most recent scheduled backup is more than 24 hours old, take an ad-hoc one before proceeding; (2) PATCH `/api/feature-flags/:key` to disable the flag; (3) verify, per the specific flag's row in Chapter 8 §8.5, that the pilot radiologist's open draft(s) fall back to the legacy path cleanly — for `render_v2`/`voice_structured` this means draining (signing or explicitly re-saving) any draft touched by the flag before it is disabled fleet-wide, not just for the pilot user; (4) confirm no signed report was touched (umbrella invariant, D-19/D-20); (5) log the drill outcome, timestamp, and whether it was a rehearsal or a real incident in the changelog entry (C-17). |
| **Chapter-8 flags in motion** | `structured_core` (A) is the first flag to reach Pilot, alone, before anything else starts even Internal stage (Chapter 8 §8.3). Later in the program, each Tier 1/2/3/4 flag repeats this same Pilot stage on its own schedule once its dependencies are proven — Pilot is not a single calendar event for the whole platform, it recurs once per flag. |

**Daily feedback loop.** Every reading day during Pilot, a 15-minute checkpoint between the pilot radiologist and the engineering owner: the radiologist reports anything that felt wrong, slow, or surprising (not limited to bugs — workflow friction counts); engineering reviews the prior 24 hours of the shadow-diff log, the error log for the flagged path, and the A5 drift scan. Every item raised is logged with a resolution or an explicit "carried to next day" status; C-17's changelog entry for the flag names how many checkpoint items were raised and their resolution status at exit. A checkpoint with an unresolved carried-over item blocks the next day's continued Pilot use of the surface until resolved or explicitly waived by the Chief Clinical Architect.

### 12.5 Stage — Department

Every radiologist and typist on the single Synology deployment (Chapter 8 §8.4's Department row). This is where the ten v1 study types (B-29) get cut over from whatever the radiologist was using before to the Reporting Workspace's full pipeline, one wave at a time.

| | |
|---|---|
| **Entry criteria** | Chapter 8 §8.4 Department row: Pilot exit criteria met; every flag this feature depends on (§8.3) at Department stage or beyond. Deployment-specific: the per-modality cutover wave about to start (below) has its SOP addendum written and every radiologist in that wave has completed the training checklist (below) and signed it. |
| **Exit criteria** | Chapter 8 §8.4 Department row: minimum 10 reading days department-wide; turnaround-time metrics (Chapter 14) not regressed against the pre-flag baseline; drift/error rate at or below Internal-stage baseline. Deployment-specific: every modality wave scheduled for this Department-stage window has completed its own cutover and training sign-off. |
| **Monitoring signals** | Chapter 14's turnaround-time dashboard, department-wide lint gutter-mark rates, studies-per-radiologist-per-hour compared against each radiologist's pre-flag baseline (no fixed cross-radiologist target — case mix varies too much for one number), error rate (target: no increase over pre-flag baseline). |
| **Rollback procedure** | Same kill-switch mechanism as Pilot (§12.4), scaled up: because Department stage is "globally live" with real patients across every modality wave already cut over, the drain-open-drafts step (for `render_v2`/`voice_structured`) now applies department-wide, not to one radiologist — coordinate the disable with a brief announcement so nobody starts a new draft mid-drain. A same-day backup (Chapter 9/D-35) is mandatory before any Department-stage rollback, no exception. |
| **Chapter-8 flags in motion** | `structured_core` (A) reaches Department first, alone; Tier 1 flags begin their own Internal→Pilot→Department progression once A clears Department (Chapter 8 §8.3 and build order). The per-modality wave sequence below is independent of which strand flags are live — it governs the *visible* Reporting Workspace surface (Quick Select, lint, normal templates), most of which already exists pre-strand; the strand flags govern the *structured-data backend* underneath it. |

**Per-modality enablement order (decided here — no source document specifies one).** The Reporting Workspace route-forward cutover (D-01's migration impact: routing radiologists from the legacy pages into the Workspace) is sequenced per modality by worklist routing configuration, not switched for all ten study types simultaneously. Order, ascending by critical-registry density (B-07) and structural-gate complexity (B-05, D-08):

| Wave | Modalities | Rationale |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `cxr` | Highest volume, simplest field set, only 2 critical-registry entries (tension/complete pneumothorax, massive effusion with shift) — proves the whole pipeline (tiles, lint, sign gate, escalation) at lowest stakes. |
| 2 | `usg_abdomen`, `usg_kub` | Next volume tier; 2 critical entries (free gas under diaphragm, pyonephrosis); Quick Select already most mature here from prior ticketed work (D-01). |
| 3 | `hrct_chest` | No direct critical-registry entries but higher field density (CTSS composite scoring) — a complexity ramp without added escalation load. |
| 4 | `doppler_ll` | Dual-template study (venous/arterial, D-17); 2 critical entries (acute femoro-popliteal DVT, critical limb ischemia) — first wave to exercise the escalation workflow (B-07) under real load after wave 1 proved it lightly. |
| 5 | `ct_brain`, `mri_brain` | Highest critical-registry density of any wave (ICH all types, midline shift ≥5 mm — the hard measurement-triggered block, large-territory infarct, diffuse cerebral edema); enabled only once the escalation workflow is proven in wave 4. |
| 6 | `mri_ls_spine`, `mri_c_spine` | Acute cord compression escalation plus the qualitative canal-stenosis grading contract (D-08); the `classification` flag's suggested-grade ghost, if live by this point, gets its first real spine exposure here. |
| 7 | `mammography` | Last: BI-RADS 5 critical trigger fires from the scale side (D-06), mandatory scale↔management binding (B-05) blocks sign on any mismatch, ACR composition is a mandatory pre-sign gate, dense-breast rider auto-appends — the highest structural-compliance burden of the ten, enabled only once the lint/blocking machinery is proven across every other wave. |

**Training/SOP integration.** Each wave gets a short SOP addendum (one page: what changed, the new keyboard map defaults per D-38, the ✕/△/◌ gutter-mark meanings, how to reach the coordinator if something looks wrong) and a training checklist every radiologist in that wave signs before their worklist routing switches them to the Workspace for that modality — legacy routes remain reachable (D-01, D-27: no route deletions) throughout, so a radiologist who hits a wall can fall back manually while the issue is triaged. Because `feature_flags` has no per-modality scope, this wave sequencing is enforced procedurally (worklist routing configuration + coordinator instruction), exactly as Chapter 8 §8.4 already notes "pilot" and "department" are procedural, not technical, scopes.

### 12.6 Stage — Hospital

All hospital staff whose workflows touch the surface: radiologists, typists, coordinators, technologists at the worklist, and referring clinicians receiving outputs.

| | |
|---|---|
| **Entry criteria** | Chapter 8 §8.4 Hospital row: Department exit criteria met; explicit clinical-owner sign-off recorded in the changelog for every BINDING-tagged dependency (D-19 through D-37 as applicable). Deployment-specific: the `dicom_pull_jobs (status, created_at)` index (missing per the module inventory) is added before Hospital stage begins for any flag touching DICOM ingestion volume, since Hospital-stage load is the first point this gap becomes load-bearing; the `pg` pool `max: 20` setting (C-18) is confirmed explicit in configuration, not left at the driver default of 10; the B-28 remediation (§12.2) for hardcoded viewer/PACS addresses is complete; a written, drilled downtime SOP (below) exists and has been rehearsed at least once. |
| **Exit criteria** | Chapter 8 §8.4 Hospital row: one full release cycle (minimum 4 weeks) with no safety incident attributable to the flagged path; the flag is now load-bearing (§8.5's rollback caution intensifies accordingly). |
| **Monitoring signals** | `pg` pool saturation (connections in use vs. `max: 20`); DIMSE pull-agent queue depth and failed-retrieval-queue size; WADO-URI proxy latency under concurrent viewer load; referring-clinician output delivery success rate (below); the B-07 escalation-communication scorecard under full hospital volume, not just pilot/department volume. |
| **Rollback procedure** | Same kill-switch mechanism, now explicitly hospital-wide: a Hospital-stage rollback is a coordinated event — coordinator broadcast before the PATCH, drain window for any drain-required flag (F, K), mandatory pre-rollback backup (Chapter 9/D-35), and a post-rollback verification pass against `/api/system/version` and `/api/health/schema` (§12.1) before declaring the rollback complete. |
| **Chapter-8 flags in motion** | By the time any flag reaches Hospital stage, every flag it depends on has also reached Hospital (Chapter 8 §8.3's "hospital requires all dependencies at hospital" rule). `render_v2` (F) and `ai_assist` (M) are, by construction, the last two flags to reach Hospital stage — expect Hospital-stage rollout to be dominated by Tier 1/2 flags for most of this stage's duration, with F and M arriving only near the end. |

**Integration load.** PACS/Orthanc and the DIMSE pull agent run independently of the `care-api`/`care-web` Compose lifecycle (they are separate containers on the same NAS, per the module inventory's "single Synology NAS is SPOF for web+API+Postgres+Orthanc+OHIF+Conquest") — a `care-api` deploy outage (§12.1) does not stop image acquisition, but it does stop reporting, worklist updates, and any referring-clinician-facing output for its 3–5 minute duration. Hospital-stage load is the first point where this matters operationally: schedule deploys for the lowest-volume hour of the day and announce them, per the downtime SOP below, exactly as if they were planned downtime, because to a referring clinician they are indistinguishable from one.

**Referring-clinician-facing outputs.** Signed report delivery (PDF, portal, or the existing WhatsApp/SMS integration channel per C-22's pattern) draws only from `patient_reports.body` (D-19, the authoritative signed record) — never from a draft, never from any AI-suggestion surface. Per D-32, the clinical body a referring clinician receives is clean: no inline AI markers, no confidence bands, no provenance data — the "AI Draft — Requires Radiologist Review" label only ever appears inside the Reporting Workspace, before sign, never in a delivered output. Delivery timestamp and channel are recorded in the report's audit trail; a failed delivery (channel down, number invalid) surfaces to the coordinator ledger, never silently drops.

**Downtime procedures (decided here — no source document specifies one; grounded in the confirmed single-NAS SPOF, CTO §4.9/T3).** Because the NAS is a single point of failure for web, API, Postgres, Orthanc, and the viewer stack together, Hospital stage cannot go live without a written, drilled downtime SOP:

1. **Declaration.** Any staff member who cannot reach the worklist for more than 5 minutes notifies the coordinator, who declares a downtime state and broadcasts it (phone tree or hospital PA, not an in-app notification — the app is what's down).
2. **Fallback reporting.** Radiologists revert to the pre-CARE manual/verbal reporting process for the duration; technologists continue image acquisition normally since Orthanc/Conquest are independent containers and may still be reachable even when `care-api`/`care-web` are not — verify this per-incident, since a full NAS outage takes everything down together.
3. **Critical results.** Any critical finding identified during downtime is escalated by direct phone call using the same referring-doctor contact list the in-app escalation workflow would have used (C-22's phone-call attestation path), logged on paper with a timestamp, and entered into the escalation audit trail retroactively once the system is back, explicitly flagged "logged after the fact."
4. **Restoration.** On restore: confirm `care-migrate` and `care-db-patch-v2`/`care-schema-verify` exited 0 in `docker logs`; confirm `/api/system/version` and `/api/health/schema` report the expected build and clean migration state (§12.1); confirm the audit hash-chain did not fork (D-34's advisory-lock protection) before allowing new sign actions; reconcile every paper-logged critical result into the system per step 3.
5. **Post-incident.** A downtime incident of any duration is a changelog entry (C-17's pattern extended to incidents, not just flag PATCHes) naming duration, cause, and whether any critical result was delayed past the B-07 five-minute scorecard target as a result.

**Why Hospital stage does not get a zero-downtime deploy path.** The CTO review flags `deploy-synology.sh`'s teardown-and-rebuild (§12.1) as a HIGH finding precisely because schema mutation happens inside the outage window with no expand/contract discipline. This chapter does not fix that — it is a Chapter 9/engineering-process concern, not a rollout-stage concern — but Hospital stage is the point at which its cost stops being an engineering inconvenience and starts being a clinical-operations fact: every code deploy (not flag flip) at Hospital stage and beyond is scheduled and communicated exactly like planned downtime (step 1 of the SOP above), even though it is shorter, because to hospital staff and referring clinicians the two are indistinguishable. A future zero-downtime path (blue/green, expand/contract) is out of this chapter's scope and belongs to Chapter 9 or a dedicated infrastructure strand; until it exists, "deploy" and "brief planned downtime" are the same operational event and must be staffed as one.

### 12.7 Stage — Multi-center

Applies only once a second physical hospital/site is onboarded. Nothing in this stage is scheduled until Hospital stage at the first site has run clean for at least one full quarter (aligning with the B-27 quarterly governance loop) and the prerequisites below are met — this is explicitly Should-tier, not yet in motion, work.

**Topology decision (decided here, consistent with conflict-register principle 5 and the CTO's T1/D1 finding that the schema is not tenant-ready).** Multi-center is a **federated deployment**, not a shared multi-tenant database: each site runs its own complete stack — own Synology NAS, own `care-db` Postgres instance, own PACS/Orthanc, own Docker Compose lifecycle — provisioned and staged through the exact same Internal→Pilot→Department→Hospital progression as site 1, from zero, with **no carryover of stage maturity between sites** (Chapter 8 §8.4's Multi-center row: a flag proven at Hospital stage on site 1 does not carry over to site 2). A single shared database with logical tenant partitioning across all 319 existing `serial int4`-keyed tables is the CTO's 🔴-rated redesign path (§4.10) and is explicitly out of scope for this chapter — it is Chapter 15 (future-roadmap) territory, gated behind a dedicated identity/tenancy strand that does not exist yet. Federation avoids that redesign entirely: it gives each site full blast-radius isolation (a rollback, an incident, or even a full restore at one site touches nothing at another) at the cost of no live cross-site query surface, which the current use case does not need.

**Central vs. local catalog governance.** "Central" is the git repository of YAML content packs plus the governance process (D-05: severity scales, scale↔management bindings, the critical registry, blocking lint rules, and escalation behavior are centrally governed with clinical sign-off) — one canonical `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` catalog version (B-03) that every site eventually runs. "Local" is the C-24 practice-wording overlay table, which this chapter extends with a nullable `branch_id` dimension (additive, B-23) on top of its existing `id_key + field` key, so a site's wording customizations never collide with another site's. The D-05 boundary holds without modification at multi-center scale: no overlay, at any site, may weaken or remove a centrally-governed item — the stricter reading (safety principle 1) applies per-site exactly as it applies per-practice today.

**Site-specific packs — there are none.** Sites do not fork the YAML packs; that would violate the immutable-keys doctrine (B-01) and fragment the one-catalog-seven-consumers model. Site differentiation is entirely: (1) the `branch_id`-scoped C-24 overlay rows (wording only); (2) the `ff_radiology_presets` (J) resolution order, whose existing Doctor → Department → Hospital tiers are simply re-scoped by `branch_id` once it exists — no rename needed, since "Hospital" already meant "this site's default" and now there is more than one hospital to mean it about; (3) `pacs_settings`/`clinic_settings` rows, which are already per-deployment since each site is its own stack.

**Sync architecture.** There is no runtime database-to-database sync between sites. `ff_radiology_catalog_delta` (H) is what each site uses to pull catalog updates from the centrally-versioned git packs into its own running system without a full redeploy (Chapter 8's description of H) — at multi-center scale, this simply means every site runs its own instance of that same mechanism against the same git source, independently and asynchronously. A site that hasn't redeployed in a while may briefly run an older `PATCH`-level catalog version than another site; this is tolerated (B-03's semver contract is exactly what makes that safe) but is a governance-staleness signal (below), not a defect, unless the gap exceeds one `MINOR` version for more than 30 days.

| | |
|---|---|
| **Entry criteria** | Site 1 at Hospital stage, stable for ≥1 quarter; `feature_flags.branch_id` (nullable) shipped and `PATCH /api/feature-flags/:key` accepts an optional branch scope (Chapter 8 §8.4's stated multi-center prerequisite — build this before scheduling a second site, not after); C-24 overlay table's `branch_id` extension shipped; B-28 remediation (§12.2) fully complete, since a second site is exactly the scenario that turns a hardcoded LAN IP into a cross-site outage; downtime SOP (§12.6) proven at site 1. |
| **Exit criteria (per new site)** | The new site independently clears its own Internal → Pilot → Department → Hospital stages (§12.3–§12.6) in full — there is no shortcut. |
| **Monitoring signals** | Per-site `catalog_version` drift (alert past one MINOR version / 30 days, above); per-site turnaround-time and error-rate parity dashboards (Chapter 14); C-24 overlay-row growth per site as a governance-health proxy (heavy local customization is a signal to review, not necessarily a problem). |
| **Rollback procedure** | Because sites are physically federated, a site-level rollback is a **decommission-in-place**, not a data operation affecting other sites: disable that site's `care-web`/`care-api` ingress, leave `care-db` and its backups intact for medico-legal retention (C-11, ≥7 years) — nothing cascades to another site's stack. |
| **Chapter-8 flags in motion** | Every flag re-runs its own Internal→Hospital ladder at the new site once that site reaches the relevant clinical stage; `scale_partition` (N) is evaluated independently per site against that site's own row-count/size sentinel (Chapter 9) — a busy site 1 crossing the partitioning threshold says nothing about a newly onboarded site 2. |

**Site onboarding checklist (summary — depth belongs to Chapter 15's future-roadmap material once this stage is actually scheduled):** provision NAS and deploy the stack fresh from an empty schema (own `care-db`); assign `branch_id`; configure PACS/Orthanc with that site's own LAN addresses via env/admin settings only (§12.2, never a copy-pasted hardcoded value from site 1); import catalog packs at whatever `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` is centrally current at onboarding time; seed empty `branch_id`-scoped overlay rows; populate the escalation contact list (C-22) from that site's own doctors-table rows; run the full Internal→Hospital progression from zero.

### Acceptance criteria

- Every stage transition (Internal→Pilot, Pilot→Department, Department→Hospital) for `ff_radiology_structured_core` has a dated changelog entry naming which of this chapter's deployment-specific entry criteria (synthetic-data marking, shadow-mode run, training sign-off, downtime SOP rehearsal) were satisfied, cross-referencing Chapter 8's flag-level criteria rather than duplicating them.
- `is_synthetic` exists on `dicom_studies` and `radiology_worklist`, defaults null, and every Chapter 14 dashboard query, turnaround-time calculation, and referring-clinician-output query demonstrably filters it out — verified by a review checklist item, not just code review discretion.
- No literal LAN IP, viewer URL, or credential exists in application source after the B-28 remediation (§12.2) lands; `viewerService.ts`'s three named hardcoded values are gone and their equivalents are admin-configurable with backfilled defaults.
- The shadow-mode sub-mode (§12.3) exists and has produced a reviewed divergence log for every flag with a text- or grade-producing surface before that flag exits Internal stage.
- The per-modality wave table (§12.5) is the order actually used in worklist routing configuration — verified against the routing config, not assumed from this document.
- A downtime SOP drill (§12.6) has been run at least once, for real or as a rehearsal, before Hospital stage begins, with the outcome logged.
- The `feature_flags.branch_id` column and scoped-PATCH support exist and are verified working before any second-site onboarding date is put on a calendar (Chapter 8 §8.4's stated prerequisite, restated here as a hard gate).
- No site-specific fork of any YAML content pack exists in the repository; all site differentiation is via `branch_id`-scoped overlay rows and preset resolution only.

### Build order

1. Confirm the deploy pipeline (§12.1: container ordering, `/api/system/version`, `/api/health/schema`) matches production before scheduling any stage work — this chapter assumes it, per Chapter 9's build order item 1 (entrypoint fail-closed fixes) having already landed.
2. Ship the `is_synthetic` columns on `dicom_studies` and `radiology_worklist` (§12.1) and wire the dashboard/analytics filter before seeding any synthetic study — Internal stage cannot start cleanly without this.
3. Complete the B-28 remediation (§12.2) for the three known hardcoded-address violations before Hospital stage begins for any flag; this can proceed in parallel with Internal/Pilot-stage work on other flags.
4. Build the shadow-logging sub-mode (§12.3) as part of each Tier 3/4 flag's own implementation ticket (`classification`, `render_v2`, `voice_structured`, `ai_assist`), not as a separate shared component — it needs access to each flag's specific legacy-vs-new code paths.
5. Run `ff_radiology_structured_core` through Internal→Pilot→Department per §12.3–§12.5 before any other flag starts Internal, per Chapter 8 §8.3's root-flag rule.
6. Sequence the seven modality waves (§12.5) through Department stage in the stated order, each with its SOP addendum and training sign-off, independent of which strand flags happen to be live underneath.
7. Add the `dicom_pull_jobs (status, created_at)` index and confirm the `pg` pool `max: 20` setting explicitly before Hospital stage entry criteria are considered met (§12.6).
8. Write and rehearse the downtime SOP (§12.6) before Hospital stage begins; this is a prerequisite, not a parallel-track item.
9. Do not begin any multi-center (§12.7) work — including the `feature_flags.branch_id` column — until site 1 has run at Hospital stage for a full quarter; when it is time, ship `branch_id` on `feature_flags` and the C-24 overlay extension first, as the two build-order blockers Chapter 8 and this chapter both name.

---

## Chapter 13 — Analytics architecture

This chapter owns the pipeline that turns the reporting loop's state transitions into the six mandated product analytics, and it owns the quarterly content/AI governance loop (B-27) end to end: what triggers it, what it consumes, and who acts on its output. It does not emit events itself — Chapters 1, 2, 4, and 7 each emit into the store this chapter defines the consumption side of — and it does not re-litigate `radiology_analytics_events`'s physical creation, which Chapter 9 (Database Evolution) already ships via the two D-36-sanctioned migration paths; this chapter is the conceptual and consumption owner Chapter 9 names when it says "Chapter [Analytics] consumes it; this chapter creates it." The overriding constraint on everything below is P1: analytics is instrumentation on top of the reporting loop, never a dependency of it — no analytics write, read, or aggregation job may add latency to typing, dictation, tile-fire, sign, or any other action on the critical path. This chapter also resolves three gaps no source document closes: the raw-event retention/aggregation-cadence split, the never-fired-tile lookback window, and the access-governance model for radiologist-identifying quality data — each is stated as a decision, not left open.

### Event capture

**Two capture lanes, chosen by whether the action already round-trips to the server.** Every analytics-relevant action falls into exactly one of two lanes; an implementer must classify a new event into one of these before wiring it, never invent a third.

- **Lane A — transactional, server-authoritative.** Per-gesture actions that already hit the server as part of their own clinical or content transaction: `finding.fired`/`confirmed`/`removed`/`signed`/`escalation_started`/`escalation_completed`/`amended` (Chapter 2's event contract), recommendation selection/edit/deletion/dedup events (Recommendation usage, below — this chapter states the contract Chapter 4 deferred), template/normal-template application, and any `ai_suggestion_audit` disposition change (Chapter 7). These events are written in the **same database transaction** as the source-of-truth mutation they describe (the `report_finding_instances` insert, the `patient_reports` finalize, the `ai_suggestion_audit` update). This is safe for the input loop because these are human-gesture-rate actions (a save, a sign, a card click), not per-keystroke events — one extra insert in a transaction that is already a server round-trip is immaterial, and transactional consistency means the quarterly governance loop and the standing scorecard never need to reconcile a lossy side-channel against the tables that already are the source of truth.
- **Lane B — client-batched, best-effort.** Per-interaction UI telemetry that is *not* already a server round-trip and must never become one: tile hover/search-select before a fire, command-line keystrokes short of an invocation, free-text-span signatures (Template usage, below), keyboard-alias usage, and Quick Select ranking impressions. These are buffered client-side in memory and flushed as a single batch **every 20 seconds or 50 buffered events, whichever comes first, plus on study-close or tab-hide** via a non-blocking request (`fetch` with `keepalive`, not `sendBeacon`-dependent — the deployment has no CDN edge to make that meaningfully faster). The flush never awaits a response before the UI continues; a failed flush is logged client-side, retried exactly once on the next flush cycle, and then dropped. This is a deliberate asymmetry with Chapter 7's audit-write rule (fail loudly, never swallow): Lane B is telemetry, not the AI audit trail or a clinical record, and B-10's unconditional-recording rule does not apply to it — `ai_suggestion_audit` itself is Lane A and keeps its fail-loud contract unchanged.

**Ingestion surface.** Lane B batches post to one endpoint, `POST /api/radiology-analytics/events`, which performs a single multi-row insert into `radiology_analytics_events` and nothing else — no synchronous aggregation, no synchronous governance-loop evaluation. Lane A events are written by an in-process helper called from the owning route/service (not a second HTTP hop) inside the caller's existing transaction. Both lanes land in the same table (Storage, below) with the same event envelope, so the aggregation layer never needs to know which lane an event arrived by.

**What is never captured.** No keystroke-level event exists for typed report text (violates P1 outright and would duplicate PHI-bearing content into a second store, see Template usage's free-text handling). No analytics write blocks a save-draft, a sign, an AI-suggestion render, or a viewer action. No analytics failure is ever surfaced to the radiologist as an error, retried against user-visible state, or allowed to affect report content or gate behavior — analytics is read-only with respect to the reporting loop; C-04's determinism rule for blocking gates is untouched by anything in this chapter.

### Storage

**`radiology_analytics_events`** (Chapter 9's table; owned physically there, consumed here) is the narrow, write-heavy event log for both lanes: `event_type` (dotted name), `occurred_at`, `actor` (server-derived per D-33, null for pre-authentication events, never client-supplied), `study_ref`/`worklist_ref`/`draft_id`/`report_id` (nullable), `catalog_key` (the stable join dimension, never a display label, per B-01), a nullable `branch_id` (D-37 prospective adoption — inert in the single-hospital deployment, schema-ready for multi-center per-site breakdowns without a later ALTER), and a `payload` jsonb column for event-specific dimensions. It is never read by any clinical or safety-gating code path.

**`ai_suggestion_audit` is not duplicated into `radiology_analytics_events`.** Chapter 7 already states its dashboards "read the same table; there is no separate aggregation pipeline to keep in sync" — this chapter honors that: every AI-suggestion-derived metric below (recommendation acceptance/edit/override, edit distance, lint-dismissal patterns) queries `ai_suggestion_audit` directly, keyed by `surface`/`suggestion_class`/`disposition`/`model_version`. `radiology_analytics_events` carries only the events that have no other home: finding-lifecycle, catalog/tile, recommendation, and template-usage transitions.

**Raw-event retention closes the gap Chapter 9 left open.** Chapter 9 states `radiology_analytics_events` is "the one candidate table where partitions genuinely age out and are dropped after aggregation" and defers the number to this chapter. The number: **raw partitions are retained 13 months** (one full year of trend plus a one-quarter buffer, enough for every quarterly governance comparison in this chapter to run year-over-year without touching a dropped partition) and a monthly partition may only be dropped **after** its contents are reflected in `radiology_analytics_rollups` (below) by the monthly finalize job — never on a bare calendar trigger. This is the same discipline as Chapter 9's own exclusion-list pattern: the drop job's precondition is "rollup finalized for this month," checked, not assumed.

**`radiology_analytics_rollups` — new table, this chapter's primary storage contribution.** A small, durable, indefinitely-retained aggregate table, created via one of the two D-36-sanctioned paths and following D-37 identity rules (`bigint` identity PK, nullable `tenant_id`/`branch_id`). One row per `(metric_name, dimension_set, period_month)` — `dimension_set` is a small fixed jsonb (catalog_key, study_type, radiologist_id, branch_id, model_version — whichever the metric uses; never free text, never raw PHI). It carries pre-aggregated counts/rates/medians, not raw events. It is **not** a partitioning candidate under Chapter 9's sentinel — like `radiology_catalog_*`, its row count is small and slow-growing (one row per metric per dimension combination per month, not per event) — this chapter states that exclusion explicitly so no future engineer schedules it into the partitioning ladder by mistake. Populated by two jobs: a **nightly incremental rollup** (extends the current month's rows from the day's new raw events, cheap, idempotent — re-running it for the same day must not double-count) and a **monthly finalize job** (closes the month, is the precondition for that month's raw partition becoming drop-eligible). Dashboards read `radiology_analytics_rollups` for any window older than the trailing 30 days, and read the live tables (`radiology_analytics_events`, `ai_suggestion_audit`, `report_finding_instances`) directly for the trailing 30 days — mirroring Chapter 7's "no separate pipeline" philosophy for recent windows while giving quarterly and year-over-year views a stable, small, indefinitely-retained surface to query.

**`radiology_missed_finding_reviews` — new table, sensitive, workflow-carrying.** Missed Findings (below) needs review state (open / reviewed / rule-gap-filed), not just a count, so it does not fit the flat event log or the rollup table. Detailed in Missed findings.

**Migration source, not target shape.** As Chapter 9 already states, `radiology_user_item_usage_logs` (legacy tile-usage/recents log) predates this chapter's event contract and is a one-time migration source only, never the shape new code writes to.

### Finding frequency (per study type, per radiologist, per site)

**Events.** `finding.fired` (all four entry mechanisms, per Chapter 2) and `finding.signed`, both carrying `catalog_key`, study/modality, `actor`, and the inert `branch_id`.

**Aggregation.** Monthly rollup rows keyed by `(catalog_key, study_type, radiologist_id, branch_id)` counting fires and signed-confirmations separately (a fired-then-removed finding is frequency-relevant for tile-ranking purposes but not for the clinical-frequency table, which counts only what reached a signed report). This is the same fire-count data the quarterly governance loop reads for never-fired-tile detection (Template usage) and for per-user tile ranking (Template usage) — one set of monthly rollup rows serves three consumers.

**Who sees it.** Catalog-level, study-type-level aggregates (no radiologist dimension) are visible on the content-governance dashboard to any content editor — this is case-mix epidemiology, not individual performance data, and carries no access restriction beyond normal staff authentication. Per-radiologist finding frequency (a radiologist's own case mix) is visible to that radiologist unrestricted in My Analytics. A **named, cross-radiologist** frequency breakdown is restricted to the clinical-owner/quality-committee role (the same gate as Radiologist variation, below) because a rare finding attributed to one radiologist in a narrow time window can be re-identifying for the underlying patient even without a patient identifier in the row.

**Privacy/fairness constraint.** Any cross-tab that combines `catalog_key` + `radiologist_id` + a time window narrow enough to produce a cell count below **10** is suppressed (rendered as "insufficient volume," never a zero or a rounded estimate) before it reaches any UI, self-view included — this is a minimum-cell-size rule applied uniformly across all six analytics wherever a radiologist- or site-level dimension is present, stated once here and referenced by number below rather than re-derived per section.

### Radiologist variation (phrasing/severity/recommendation variation for the same finding)

**Scope, decided.** V1 measures variation on the three structured axes the catalog already makes comparable — **severity value chosen** (the `sev.*` key), **recommendation code chosen** (`rec.*`), and **free-text-edit-after-accept rate** on that finding's rendered sentence (a proxy for phrasing drift) — for the same `catalog_key`. True phrasing-similarity clustering across radiologists' free text is explicitly **Future tier**: no source document specifies a governance-approved mechanism for comparing radiologists' prose to one another, and building one is a materially heavier privacy and governance decision (it requires comparing clinical free text across staff, not just counting structured choices) than this chapter can adjudicate unilaterally. This is stated as a scoping decision, not an oversight.

**Events.** `finding.signed` payload carries the chosen `severity` and `recommendation_code` alongside `catalog_key` and `actor`; the free-text-edit proxy reuses `ai_suggestion_audit`'s `accepted-then-edited` disposition filtered to `suggestion_class` values that resolve to a `catalog_key`'s bound sentence, plus the non-AI equivalent — a finding whose rendered sentence was edited after tile/macro insertion, captured as a `finding.text_edited_after_insert` Lane A event at save-draft time (edited vs. verbatim, not the diff itself, to avoid storing a second copy of comparative clinical prose per the scoping decision above).

**Aggregation.** Monthly rollup rows per `(catalog_key, radiologist_id)`: distribution of `severity` values chosen, distribution of `recommendation_code` chosen, and the text-edit rate — each compared against the practice-wide median/mode for that `catalog_key` in the same period. A radiologist's distribution is only computed and shown once their fired-and-signed count for that `catalog_key` in the window reaches **20** (a higher floor than the general 10-cell suppression rule, because a *distribution* is more inference-sensitive than a raw count and this chapter deliberately avoids drawing calibration conclusions from a subspecialist's or part-timer's thin case mix).

**Surfaced how — the governance split, decided here.** Three visibility tiers, none of which is a cross-radiologist leaderboard:
1. **Self-view (My Analytics).** A radiologist sees their own distribution against the de-identified practice median — never against a named peer, never ranked.
2. **Committee-view (clinical-owner / quality-committee role only).** Named, per-radiologist breakdowns, used exclusively for calibration education and outlier review as an input to the quarterly safety review (B-27, §16.5's pattern extended from registry/lint/escalation to variation). Every open of the named-view is a governance-sensitive action logged to the `audit_logs` chain (same rigor as D-34's audit hardening), independent of and in addition to normal page-view logging.
3. **Never:** a public or peer-visible leaderboard; any feed into compensation, credentialing, or automated disciplinary action. This chapter states as a hard rule — enforced by keeping the committee-view behind its own role check, never behind the general practice-dashboard role — that variation data may only trigger a human-led calibration conversation, never an automated consequence. This mirrors B-12's personalization firewall in spirit (corrections/variation inform education, never rewrite safety systems) even though B-12 itself is about AI learning, not human peer review.

### Recommendation usage (adherence, edit rates, dedup effectiveness)

**Events — this chapter states the contract Chapter 4 deferred.** Chapter 4 (Recommendation Lifecycle) names "recommendation-deletion counts" as telemetry it feeds to this chapter without defining the event shape; the contract:

| Event | Fires on | Key dimensions |
|---|---|---|
| `recommendation.selected` | A `rec.*` code is bound to a signed finding instance (fixed or templated) | `rec_code`, `catalog_key`, `family` (if any), `actor` |
| `recommendation.edited` | The rendered `rec.*` line is free-text-overridden before sign (a lint-flagged path per B-06 — free-text advice is a lint finding) | `rec_code`, `edited: true` |
| `recommendation.deleted` | A bound recommendation is removed before sign | `rec_code`, `catalog_key`, `actor` |
| `recommendation.dedup_applied` | Chapter 4's merge engine collapses duplicates | `rule` ∈ {identity, family, urgent_specialist_event}, `family` (if family-rule), count of lines collapsed |

For AI-*suggested* recommendations specifically, acceptance/edit/override rates are not re-captured here — they are the `suggestion_class = rec.*` slice of Chapter 7's `ai_suggestion_audit`, queried directly, per Storage's no-duplication rule.

**Aggregation.** Monthly rollups per `(rec_code, family)`: adherence rate (`selected` reaching sign unedited / total `selected`), edit rate (`edited` / total rendered), deletion rate (`deleted` / total bound), and dedup effectiveness (average lines collapsed per report via `dedup_applied`, broken out by rule). Per-radiologist deletion/edit rates exist as a further breakdown but are gated by the same committee-view rule as Radiologist variation, since a high personal deletion rate on a specific `rec_code` is itself a variation signal about that radiologist's practice pattern, not just a content-quality signal.

**Who sees it.** Catalog-level adherence/edit/dedup rates (no radiologist dimension) are visible to content editors and feed B-27's "frequently deleted recommendations trigger quarterly wording review" directly: any `rec_code` whose practice-wide deletion rate exceeds a fixed **15%** threshold in a quarter is auto-nominated onto the content editor's wording-review queue (a proposal, not an automatic edit — a human content editor still decides, per the catalog-lifecycle chapter's promotion gate). Per-radiologist breakdowns follow Radiologist variation's committee-view gate.

**Privacy/fairness.** Catalog-level dedup effectiveness and adherence carry no re-identification risk and are unrestricted. Per-radiologist deletion-rate views apply the 10-cell floor from Finding frequency at minimum, and the 20-instance floor from Radiologist variation where a distribution (not a single rate) is shown.

### Template usage (pack/entry/tile usage, dead-content detection)

**Tile and combo fires are not a separate event.** `tile.fired` telemetry is `finding.fired` filtered to `source = "tile"`, and a combo tile's simultaneous member fires share a `batch_id` in the payload so combo usage is measurable as its own dimension without inventing a second event type — this chapter's contribution here is the aggregation and retirement-feed logic, not a new capture point.

**Template application is its own event, since it is not a finding.** `template.applied` fires when a radiologist applies a `tpl.normal.*` normal template or a `structured_report_templates`/`ai_normal_report_templates` preset, carrying the template's key/id, `study_type`, and `actor` (Lane A, same transaction as the apply).

**Free-text repetition — captured as a signature, not as text.** The B-27 "repeated free text → draft tile candidates" signal needs to detect that many radiologists are typing similar unstructured sentences, without creating a second long-lived store of comparative clinical prose (the same concern that scoped Radiologist variation's phrasing axis). Decision: at section-confirm or sign time (never per-keystroke), a free-text finding/impression span emits `report_text.free_text_span` carrying a normalized similarity signature (lowercased, whitespace-collapsed, tokenized) plus `study_type`, `section`, and a length bucket — never the raw sentence. The signature is sufficient to cluster candidate repeats for nomination purposes. When a cluster crosses the nomination threshold (below), the actual candidate-entry authoring step reads the real report text directly from `report_finding_instances`/`patient_reports` for the specific reports in that cluster, under the content editor's normal report-access permissions, scoped to a bounded lookback — it is never sourced from the analytics event log, so clinical free text is never duplicated into the analytics store.

**Dead-content detection and the never-fired lookback window — decided here.** A catalog entry, tile, combo, or normal/structured template is a **retirement candidate** when it has zero `finding.fired`/`template.applied` events since the later of (a) its import date or (b) the last quarterly governance review, **with a minimum content-age floor of one full quarter** before it becomes eligible — newly imported content is never flagged dead in its first review cycle. This runs as part of the monthly rollup's quarterly finalize pass and writes candidates to the content-governance queue Chapter 1 owns the UI for; Chapter 1's retirement decision (human content editor, clinical-owner sign-off for centrally-governed content per D-05) consumes this chapter's output and is out of this chapter's scope beyond producing the candidate list.

**Free-text-repetition nomination threshold.** A signature cluster nominates a draft-tile candidate when it recurs **8 or more times across 3 or more distinct radiologists** within a rolling quarter — the distinct-radiologist floor exists specifically so one radiologist's personal phrasing habit is never mistaken for a practice-wide content gap.

**Tile ranking — resolves the open question in the Seed Spec digest on how per-user analytics supersede the seeded Top-100 order.** Quick Select tile order is **per-user**, not per-practice: a new user's tiles render in the seeded Top-100 weight order (cold start); once a user has **30 or more** fires for a given study type, their personal order shifts to a frequency ranking computed from their own `finding.fired` events with **exponential decay, 60-day half-life** (so ranking tracks a radiologist's current rotation/subspecialty mix, not their lifetime history), blended with the seed weight as a smoothing prior below the 30-fire floor. Command-line frequency ranking (§7.3/§7.6 of the master spec) uses the same per-user decayed frequency for consistency between the two surfaces. A **practice-wide** aggregate ranking (count-based only, no per-radiologist detail, so it carries none of Radiologist variation's access restrictions) exists solely as the cold-start default for new hires and is recomputed from the same monthly rollup.

**Who sees it.** Catalog-level fire counts and retirement candidates: content editors. Per-user tile ranking: that user only, as their own workstation personalization — never shared, never used to judge a radiologist's practice pattern, and explicitly out of scope for the committee-view gate that governs Radiologist variation, because tile-usage pattern reflects case-mix and habit, not clinical judgment quality.

### Missed findings (retrospective flags, addenda correlation, AI-suggested-but-rejected-then-added)

**This is the most sensitive of the six analytics and is treated accordingly: restricted, workflow-carrying, and structurally incapable of becoming an automatic individual-performance signal.**

**Three source signals, correlated on a schedule, not in real time.**
1. **Addenda correlation.** A `finding.amended` event (Chapter 2, Signed → Amended) whose added content's `catalog_key` matches an entry that was, on the original study, an `ai_suggestion_audit` row with `disposition` ∈ {skipped, dismissed-with-reason, overridden} — the AI-suggested-but-rejected-then-added case named in scope.
2. **Missed-lintable-error correlation.** A `finding.amended` event whose added content corresponds to a gutter-mark (`✕`/`△`) suggestion that was dismissed (not fixed) on the original study — this is the concrete trigger for B-27's "every addendum from a missed lintable error triggers a rule-gap review."
3. **Manual retrospective flag.** A radiologist or QA reviewer, while reading a follow-up study and reviewing priors, identifies that an earlier *signed* report omitted a finding visible in retrospect. This is a deliberate human action (`missed_finding.flagged`, Lane A, carrying the flagged report's id, the flagging user, and free-text clinical rationale — this free-text is retained, unlike Radiologist variation's phrasing axis, because it is the substance of a quality-assurance review, not a comparative-prose signal), never inferred automatically from imaging.

**Correlation job, not real-time.** A **weekly** scheduled job joins signals 1 and 2 against the trailing amendment window and writes candidate rows to `radiology_missed_finding_reviews` (fields: source report/amendment id, correlation type, matched `ai_suggestion_audit`/gutter-mark row id where applicable, status ∈ {open, reviewed, rule-gap-filed, dismissed-not-applicable}, `reviewed_by`, `review_notes`, linked rule-gap ticket id). Signal 3 writes directly to the same table at flag time with status `open`. This table is additive-only, D-37-compliant, and — because rows referencing a signed report inherit that report's retention floor (D-19/C-11) — is **excluded from every purge, drop-partition, and cold-tier eligibility check** the same way `ai_suggestion_audit` rows referenced by a signed report's provenance view already are (Chapter 9's exclusion list gains this table as a peer entry, not a special case).

**Who sees it — and what it must never feed.**
- **Self-view, private, reflective.** The monthly "Your copilot" digest in My Analytics (ai-experience-spec §17) surfaces a radiologist's own dismissed-then-later-addended items to that radiologist only, framed as a reflective prompt ("2 suggestions you dismissed were later addressed in addenda — review?"), never comparative and never pushed as an interrupt (the three-channel notification model, D-23, applies here as much as anywhere: this is Ledger-tier at most, never Interrupt-tier).
- **Committee-view.** The clinical-owner/quality-committee role sees the full `radiology_missed_finding_reviews` queue, named, for the quarterly safety review and for filing rule-gap tickets. Every open of this view is an `audit_logs`-chained governance action, same as Radiologist variation's committee-view.
- **Never:** bulk analytics export, the general practice dashboard, compensation/credentialing systems, or any automated trigger. This chapter states explicitly that `radiology_missed_finding_reviews` has no export endpoint and is excluded by name from any future bulk-analytics-export feature's default scope — an engineer adding a general export must positively opt this table back in with a second, explicit governance decision, not get it by default. This administrative confidentiality posture is adopted even though formal peer-review-as-a-report-state is deprecated (D-20) — the *workflow state* is gone, the *confidentiality discipline* around missed-finding quality data is not, and this chapter is where that discipline is re-anchored now that the old peer-review tables no longer carry it.

**Retention.** ≥7 years, matching the AI audit trail floor (C-11), for any row referencing a signed report; indefinitely for rows without one only until reviewed and closed, at which point the same floor applies once linked to a report.

### Quality metrics (turnaround, edit distance, lint violations at sign, escalation latency)

**Turnaround time — two distinct metrics, both computed from existing timestamps, no new capture.** (a) **Reading TAT** = sign timestamp minus lock-acquired timestamp (`radiology_study_locks`) — this is the number the −30% sign-to-sign scorecard target (§1/§4.6) tracks. (b) **Full TAT** = sign timestamp minus study-received timestamp (`dicom_studies`) — the operational, referrer-facing number. Both are read-time aggregates over existing tables (worklist locks, `patient_reports.signed_at`); no event is emitted solely for TAT, consistent with Chapter 7's "no separate pipeline where a timestamped row already exists" pattern.

**Edit distance on AI drafts.** Computed at rollup time (not stored per-suggestion in `ai_suggestion_audit`, which stays Chapter 7's table unmodified — no cross-chapter ALTER) from `ai_suggestion_audit.output` vs. `edited_text` for `accepted-then-edited` rows, using a word-level diff distance. The result is cached into `radiology_analytics_rollups` keyed by `(surface, suggestion_class, model_version, period_month)` so the monthly finalize job computes it once rather than every dashboard load recomputing a diff over the full audit table.

**Lint violations at sign.** Read directly from `ai_suggestion_audit` filtered to `surface = gutter_mark`, grouped by lint rule (`suggestion_class`) and `disposition`: the ✕-blocking rate (how often a report reached the sign gate carrying an unresolved contradiction, which by C-04/the sign gate's own contract can only be zero at the moment of successful sign — the metric that matters is how often ✕ appeared *at all* before resolution, a proxy for rule false-positive load) and the △ dismiss-with-reason rate per rule, feeding the same quarterly registry/lint/escalation safety review named in B-27.

**Critical-finding escalation latency.** `finding.escalation_started`/`finding.escalation_completed` (Chapter 2) — median(`completed_at` − `started_at`) is the <5-minute scorecard target (B-07). Rolling monthly median, broken out by `branch_id` (inert today, schema-ready). Unlike Radiologist variation and Missed findings, this metric is **not** committee-view-restricted by default: escalation latency is a process-timing safety metric, not a judgment-quality signal, and the standing scorecard (re-measured every release per §17.4) is practice-wide visible. A per-radiologist breakdown, if ever built, would still apply the 10-cell suppression floor but is not gated behind the quality-committee role the way variation and missed-findings data are, since a slow escalation is a workflow/system finding as often as an individual one.

**Who sees it.** The full quality-metrics set forms the standing scorecard, visible to clinical owner and practice leadership. Each radiologist's own TAT and session-fatigue view (studies read, session duration, TAT drift by hour) is self-visible in My Analytics per the UX review's fatigue-pulse design — this is the same self/committee split used throughout this chapter, and it is not repeated in full here beyond noting escalation latency's narrower restriction above.

**Privacy/fairness.** The 10-cell suppression floor applies to any TAT or lint-violation breakdown that crosses radiologist × narrow-time-window. None of these metrics may be used, alone or combined, to auto-generate a performance ranking; the scorecard's purpose is regression detection at the practice/system level (a rising ✕-rate after a catalog import, a drifting median TAT after hour 4 of a shift), not individual scoring.

### Acceptance criteria

- No analytics write (Lane A or Lane B) is ever awaited by a keystroke handler, a render path, or a viewer action; Lane B flushes are fire-and-forget with a bounded single retry and silent drop on repeated failure.
- Every Lane A event listed in this chapter's and Chapter 2's event contracts is written in the same transaction as the source-of-truth mutation it describes; no Lane A event can exist without its corresponding clinical/content row, and no clinical/content row can be committed without its corresponding Lane A event also committing (same transaction, both or neither).
- `radiology_analytics_events` raw partitions are never dropped before the corresponding month's `radiology_analytics_rollups` finalize job has succeeded; this is a checked precondition in the drop job, not an assumed ordering.
- `ai_suggestion_audit`-derived metrics are computed by querying that table directly; no metric in this chapter duplicates its rows into `radiology_analytics_events` or any other table.
- Every cross-tab combining a radiologist dimension with `catalog_key`/`rec_code` and a period narrower than a quarter is suppressed below a cell count of 10 (distributions below 20 instances for Radiologist variation specifically), uniformly across self-view and committee-view.
- Radiologist variation and Missed findings named/committee views are role-gated to clinical-owner/quality-committee and each access is written to the `audit_logs` chain as a governance-sensitive action.
- `radiology_missed_finding_reviews` has no bulk-export path and is excluded, by explicit name, from any purge/drop-partition/cold-tier job's eligibility check.
- The monthly never-fired-tile candidate list, the frequently-deleted-recommendation nomination list, and the free-text-repetition draft-tile nomination list are all produced by scheduled jobs (never on-demand triggered by a content editor's page load) and land in Chapter 1's governance queue as proposals a human decides on, never an automatic retirement or creation.
- No metric in this chapter has a write path back into any lint rule, escalation trigger, confidence-band threshold, or guideline anchor — this chapter is read-only with respect to every safety system defined elsewhere, mirroring B-12's firewall.

### Build order

1. `radiology_analytics_events` and its indexes (Chapter 9, item 6) must land first — this chapter has nothing to consume before it exists.
2. Lane A emitter wiring inside Chapters 1, 2, 4, and 7's existing transactions (`emitAnalyticsEvent()` helper, one per owning route/service) — depends on each of those chapters' state machines being built, since the events are literally their transitions.
3. Lane B client buffer + `POST /api/radiology-analytics/events` ingestion endpoint — independent of step 2, can land in parallel once the table exists.
4. `radiology_analytics_rollups` table (D-36-sanctioned migration) and the nightly incremental + monthly finalize jobs — depends on step 1 only for schema, but produces meaningless output until steps 2–3 are emitting real events.
5. Finding frequency, Recommendation usage, and Template usage rollup queries and dashboards — depend on step 4 and on Chapters 1/2/4's `catalog_key`/`rec_code` vocabularies being stable (Chapter 9's stated dependency ordering).
6. `radiology_missed_finding_reviews` table plus the weekly correlation job — depends on Chapter 7's `ai_suggestion_audit` and Chapter 2's `finding.amended`/`report_amendments` being live, since it correlates against both.
7. Radiologist variation and Quality metrics dashboards, including the committee-view role gate and its `audit_logs` pairing — build last among the six, since they are read-only aggregations over everything above and their access-control correctness is easiest to verify once the underlying data is flowing and inspectable.
8. Wire the never-fired/free-text-repetition/frequently-deleted nomination outputs into Chapter 1's governance queue UI — the final integration point, owned jointly with Chapter 1's retirement tooling.

---

## Chapter 14 — Security

This chapter is the trust layer over everything Chapters 1–7 let a radiologist, resident, or the AI Copilot put into a report. It defines one audit model that every governance and clinical-content event across the catalog chapters writes through, one provenance contract that lets any element of a signed report be traced back to the catalog version, the human editor, or the AI suggestion that produced it, the signing model that turns a draft into a legal document, the hash-chaining that makes tampering with either the audit trail or a signed report detectable, and the role/permission matrix that decides who may do any of this. It does not re-derive the AI-specific audit record — Chapter 7 owns `ai_suggestion_audit` and the provenance *view* mechanics in full; this chapter owns the general audit substrate Chapter 7 writes into, the non-AI provenance half, and everything about signing, amending, and permission-gating that sits outside AI interaction. Every design choice below answers a specific CTO Pre-Implementation Risk Review finding, cited by ID, and nothing here weakens a Part 0 §0.6 safety rule.

### Audit

**One model, one table.** `audit_logs` (`lib/db/src/schema/auditLogs.ts`) is the single audit substrate for the whole ERP, radiology included.
