Every record carries: `userId` (nullable only for genuine system actions), `userName`, `role`, `action` (`login | logout | create | edit | delete | print | reprint | refund | export | approve | finalize | backup | download`, extended for radiology by `view` — see below), `module` (`radiology | audit | catalog | settings | backups | …`), `entityType`/`entityId` (the thing acted on, `entityId` stored as text to accept serials, UUIDs, or composite keys), `oldValue`/`newValue` (JSON snapshots, truncated at 64KB), `ipAddress`, `userAgent`, `reason` (mandatory for dismiss-with-reason and amendment actions), and the two chain fields `previousHash`/`chainHash`. There is no `radiology`-specific audit table for non-AI events — catalog governance changes (Chapter 1: MAJOR/MINOR/PATCH bumps, registry edits, status transitions), flag mutations (Chapter 8), sign/amend events (below), and report-lifecycle edits all write here, `module="radiology"` or `module="catalog"` (new module, added below under Permissions), so that "who changed what, when" across Chapters 1–7 is answered by one query against one chain, never a join across scattered logs.

**Actor identity is always server-derived.** Every `auditLog()`/`auditFromRequest()` call in the radiology surface must source `userId`/`userName`/`role` from the authenticated `staffSession` (`requireStaffAuth`), never from a request body field. This is the general form of the rule D-33 states for finalize specifically and Chapter 7 restates for suggestion actioning — this chapter is where it is stated as a blanket rule for every audit write, radiology-wide. A client-supplied actor string anywhere in an audit write is a defect, full stop.

**Hash chain (D-34, CTO CRIT-2/C4).** `auditLog()` (`artifacts/api-server/src/lib/audit.ts`) already implements the correct shape: `chainHash = SHA256(canonical JSON of {userId, userName, role, action, module, entityType, entityId, oldValue, newValue, reason, ipAddress, userAgent, createdAt, previousHash})`, with `previousHash` set to the prior row's `chainHash` (empty string for the genesis row). The read-last-row → insert critical section is wrapped in a transaction-scoped `pg_advisory_xact_lock` (Emergency Ticket E0.2, ratified by D-34) so two concurrent writers can never fork the chain — this part of CRIT-2 is landed. What remains open, and is the recorded approval for the rest of C4 per D-34, is: a **unique** index on `chain_hash` (today's `audit_chain_hash_idx` is non-unique — a forked or replayed hash is not rejected at the DB layer, only detectable after the fact by the verifier below), **DB-level append-only enforcement** (`REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE` on the table role, or a `BEFORE UPDATE OR DELETE` trigger that raises — today immutability is app-convention only: no route updates or deletes `audit_logs`, but nothing in Postgres stops a direct `UPDATE`), and a **`bigint` identity PK** (today `serial int4`, which overflows within the review's 10-year horizon on the highest-churn table in the system). All three land as the manual-only-queue migration Chapter 9 already schedules (its future-migrations item 3) — this chapter is not where the migration mechanics are decided, only where the requirement and its rationale live; see Chapter 9 for the maintenance-window procedure and the pre-change `pg_dump` it requires per D-35.

**Verification endpoint (CTO C4's "real verification endpoint").** `verifyChainRows`/`verifyAuditChain` (`artifacts/api-server/src/lib/audit.ts`) already implement a pure, correct verifier: given rows ordered by ascending `id`, it confirms each row's `previousHash` equals the immediately preceding row's `chainHash`, returning `{ ok, checked, firstBrokenId, reason }`. This function exists but is unreachable — no route calls it. Wire it as `GET /api/audit-logs/verify` (optional `?limit=` for a tail-window check), gated by the same `requireAdminRole` hard gate used for flag mutations (Permissions, below) plus an explicit `audit:verify` permission so a designated compliance officer who is not `admin`/`super_admin` can also run it. Run it three ways: (1) on-demand from this endpoint, (2) as a nightly scheduled job that writes its own `audit_logs` "system" row recording `{ ok, checked }` (the check-run itself is audit-relevant), and (3) as a mandatory step of backup restore-verification (D-35 already requires a restore-verification job; extend it to include a full chain walk before the restored environment is declared good).

**Retention.** Because the chain is one continuous structure, individual rows are never deleted — deleting any row breaks every subsequent link. The retention floor for the whole `audit_logs` table is therefore the report retention period (≥7 years, matching C-11's Indian medico-legal alignment), applied to the table as a whole rather than per-row, since a mixed-retention chain is not constructible without breaking hash continuity. When volume eventually requires archival (multi-center scale, out of this single-hospital chapter's near-term scope), that is a partitioning redesign requiring Chief Clinical Architect sign-off — Chapter 9 flags this explicitly and this chapter does not reopen it.

**Viewer access (CTO §4.5).** "Who viewed which images" currently has no server-side audit — `viewerService.ts`'s `window.open` launch is fire-and-forget with only a `localStorage` breadcrumb. Close this by writing an `audit_logs` row (`action="view"`, `module="radiology"`, `entityType="study"`) from the server-side viewer-launch endpoint at the moment a signed viewer URL is issued, not from the client after the fact — client-reported "I viewed it" events are not audit-grade. This is additive to the existing action vocabulary (`view` joins `login | logout | create | edit | delete | print | reprint | refund | export | approve | finalize | backup | download`).

### Provenance

**The contract.** Every element that ends up in a signed report — a finding, a measurement, a recommendation line, an impression fragment, a hand-typed sentence — must be traceable, for the life of the report, to three things: the **catalog version** that defined its wording/behavior at the time it was used, its **origin** (typed by a named human, or accepted from a specific AI suggestion), and the **human editor** who confirmed it into the report. This is the general form of AI Spec §19/Master Spec §12.10 (B-10) extended to non-AI content, and it is what answers CTO §4.3's "final report is one-way prose paste, no provenance" and §4.4's "structured selections have no link to the report" findings for the *structured* half of the report — the narrative-text half is covered by the render/version stamping below plus Chapter 6's fragment assembly.

**Two provenance layers, one join key.** (1) The **structured layer**: `report_finding_instances` (Chapter 3) already carries `draft_id`/`report_id` (closing §4.3's specific complaint about the legacy `radiologySmartFindings.ts` selections jsonb, which had neither — that legacy table stays frozen per the usual doctrine, its replacement already ships the FK), `finding_id` (the catalog `id_key`'s current DB pointer, re-pointing to `radiology_catalog_entries` once Chapter 1's importer lands), `catalog_version` (stamped per-instance at creation, not just per-report), `source` (`manual | ai | voice`), and `confirmed_by`/`confirmed_at` — the human editor of record for that specific finding instance, server-derived from the confirming session, never a client string. When `source="ai"`, the instance additionally carries (via Chapter 7's audit record) an `ai_suggestion_audit` row id, giving the exact suggestion, its band, its evidence, and its engine/model/prompt lineage. (2) The **narrative layer**: `radiology_report_lifecycle_log` records edit/amend/reprint events against the free-text body, giving a chronological trail of who touched the rendered text and when, independent of which structured instances backed it.

**Provenance survives rendering and signing.** This is not automatic — it is the direct consequence of the snapshot-at-sign contract (C-02, D-18): at Sign, `report_finding_instances` rows for the draft are copied with `report_id` set (the draft rows are retained, not deleted, until draft purge), and `patient_reports` is stamped, in the same transaction, with `catalogVersion`, `templateVersion`, and `renderEngineVersion` plus the `structuredJson` cache. Because this happens atomically with the write that sets the draft table's `finalReportId` (the `radiologyReportGenerator.ts` draft schema already carries this nullable FK, currently unwired — this chapter's build order wires it), a signed report's provenance is reconstructible forever from data that was frozen at the moment of signing, never from the live catalog, the live model routing config, or a since-edited draft. No provenance query for a signed report ever joins against mutable catalog state — that is precisely the failure mode C-02 exists to prevent (an old signed report reading differently because someone edited the catalog since).

**Where provenance is visible, and where it is not.** The clinical body of `patient_reports.body` stays clean — Chapter 7's rule holds generally: no inline markers beyond the governance-configured disclosure line. Provenance is exposed through a permission-gated **Report Provenance** view (Permissions, below, decides who may open it) that surfaces, per element: catalog version at time of use, origin (manual/AI/voice), editor or suggestion-id, and timestamp — reading the structured layer and, for AI-originated spans, delegating to Chapter 7's `ai_suggestion_audit` render for the deep (evidence/engine/prompt-lineage) detail. This view is read-only over data written elsewhere in this chapter and Chapter 7; it has no write path of its own.

**Identity safety is a provenance concern too (B-25).** Prior-report matching for comparison and follow-up linking is always by `patientId` (the primary key), never by name — a name-based match is exactly the kind of silent, undetectable provenance corruption (wrong patient's prior silently informing this report) that §0.6 rule 4's identity-band doctrine exists to make structurally visible instead of procedurally hoped-for. Any comparison feature (Chapter 6, Chapter 7) that resolves "the prior study" must resolve it through `patientId`, and that resolution is itself an audit-logged action (`action="view"`/`"compare"`, `entityType="report"`) so a wrong-patient linkage is forensically traceable after the fact even though the identity band is meant to prevent it before the fact.

### Signed reports

**What signing binds.** Signing is not "flip a status flag." It computes and stores a single content hash over exactly three inputs, at the moment of Sign, in one transaction: the rendered text (`patient_reports.body` as it stands at that instant), a canonical serialization of the structured JSON snapshot (`structuredJson`, keys sorted, matching the same canonical-JSON discipline `audit.ts`'s `canonicalHashPayload` already uses so the hash is reproducible), and the three catalog-version strings (`catalogVersion`, `templateVersion`, `renderEngineVersion`) stamped in the same write (D-18/C-02). This closes CTO §4.8's "signed report integrity absent" finding directly: add `patient_reports.contentHash` (new nullable text column, additive per B-23 — no retro-fill of historical rows, they read as unhashed/legacy) and populate it in the finalize transaction as `SHA256(body || canonicalJSON(structuredJson) || catalogVersion || templateVersion || renderEngineVersion)`. The existing `usg-reports.ts` finalize route's `computeHash(draftContent, patientId, accessionNumber, now, finalizedBy)` is the right *pattern* (a deterministic hash over the exact fields that must not silently change) but the wrong *inputs* for the go-forward contract — it hashes neither the structured JSON nor the catalog versions, because those columns did not exist when it was written. It is not replaced today (D-27: no route deletions); the Workspace sign pipeline that eventually supersedes it (D-27) implements the fuller hash described here.

**Who may sign.** Only the `radiologist` role, with `canFinalize=true` on `role_permissions(role="radiologist", module="radiology")`. AI never signs, under any confidence band or any degradation state (B-25, CTO §4.6's preserved positive finding, §0.6 rule 1) — there is no code path, present or future, where an `ai_suggestion_audit` disposition or a copilot action sets `patient_reports.status="signed"`. `radiology_typist` can never sign — this is the exact CTO §4.8 exploit case ("a `radiology_typist` can rewrite a signed report") and it is closed structurally, not by convention, in Permissions below. Authorship — `signedByName`, `signatureId`, `signedAt` — is written exclusively from the authenticated session at the moment `Ctrl+Enter`/voice-"sign" clears the sign gate (§0.6 rule 2, Chapter 7's gate enumeration); a client-supplied author field is rejected outright. This is CTO C3's write-path half, and it is already done correctly in one live route (`usgReports.ts`'s `finalizedBy = staffOf(req).subjectName`) and done wrong in the legacy peer-review path (`peerReview.ts:85`'s `finalizeReport(studyId, radiologistId, reportText)`, which accepts `radiologistId` from its caller rather than deriving it) — the legacy path is frozen and deprecated per D-20/D-27, not patched, and every go-forward finalize/amend route follows the correct precedent, never the legacy one.

**State machine (D-20).** `DRAFT → SIGNED → AMENDED` (repeatable). "Preliminary" and "Ready for review" are DRAFT labels, not states. The legacy `pending_verification`/`verified` two-stage path on `patient_reports.status` and the `peer_review_assignments`/`radiology_report_verifications` tables are the deprecated multi-stage-review system (Phase 17) — they stay read-only for historical rows and are never the target of new writes; nothing in this chapter reintroduces a second sign-off *state*. The resident countersign flow below reuses this exact single-sign state machine — see Permissions.

**Addendum model post-sign.** All post-sign changes go through `report_amendments` (D-19: "one amendment chain"), never a direct edit to `patient_reports.body`. Each amendment row carries `priorContent`+`priorVersionHash`, `addendumText`, `amendmentReason` (mandatory, never blank), `amendedById`/`amendedByName` (server-derived, same rule as signing), `newContent`+`newVersionHash`, `amendmentLabel` (default `"AMENDED"`), and `sequenceNumber`. This chapter fixes the chain-continuity rule the table's columns imply but nothing currently enforces: amendment 1's `priorVersionHash` must equal the signed report's `contentHash`; amendment N+1's `priorVersionHash` must equal amendment N's `newVersionHash`. The amend route validates this before insert and rejects (409) on mismatch — a gap or a forged intermediate amendment is refused at write time, not merely detectable later. Who may amend: the same `canFinalize` gate as signing — amending a signed clinical document carries the same authority weight as signing one, so there is no separate `canAmend` bit; a resident, lacking `canFinalize`, can never amend a signed report either. Every amendment writes an `audit_logs` row (`action="amend"`) and updates the provenance view.

**CTO findings closed by this section:** §4.8 (signed-report integrity, client-supplied authorship, RBAC dead code — the last is closed in Permissions), C3 (satisfied jointly with D-19/D-33), §4.6's positive finding (AI never signs) preserved.

### Tamper evidence

**Two independent chains, one verification discipline.** (1) The `audit_logs` global hash chain (Audit, above) — covers every administrative and lifecycle action. (2) The signed-report content-hash chain — `patient_reports.contentHash` as the chain's genesis link per report, continued through `report_amendments`' `priorVersionHash`/`newVersionHash` sequence (Signed reports, above). Both are SHA-256 over a canonically-serialized payload; both are designed so that tampering with any single row — editing a value, deleting a row, or inserting an out-of-order row — breaks a link that a deterministic verifier can find.

**Verification procedure.** For the audit chain: call `verifyAuditChain()` (full walk from genesis, or `{ limit }` for a tail window) — it returns the first broken row's id and a human-readable reason. For the signed-report chain: recompute `contentHash` from the stored `body`+`structuredJson`+version columns and compare to the stored value; then walk `report_amendments` in `sequenceNumber` order confirming each `priorVersionHash` equals the previous link's stored hash. Both procedures run: on-demand via the admin/audit-permission-gated endpoints, nightly on a scheduled job, and as a mandatory gate in backup restore-verification (D-35) — a restore that fails either chain-verification step is not declared a good restore.

**Response to detected tampering — decisive, since no source document specifies one.** A broken link is a CRITICAL-severity operational incident, not a data-quality warning:

1. **Never auto-repair.** The verifier only reports; nothing in this chapter, or anywhere in the codebase, may silently "re-seal" a broken chain by recomputing and overwriting hashes. This is a direct, permanent prohibition of the exact pattern CRIT-2 already flagged as dangerous ("documented boot-time blank-hash backfill can re-seal a tampered chain") — any future backfill or repair utility must refuse to run if it would overwrite a chain segment that a verification run has already recorded as broken, and any legitimate backfill (e.g. sealing genuinely-unhashed legacy rows) must operate only on rows that were never part of a verified chain in the first place, never on rows a break was detected in.
2. **Alert loudly, immediately, outside the UI toast/edge-glow vocabulary.** A detected break writes its own `audit_logs` row (`action="tamper_detected"`, `reason` = the verifier's break description) and triggers the practice's existing escalation channel (C-22's pattern: WhatsApp/SMS/email to the admin/super_admin and the practice owner) — this is a §3.6 Interrupt-channel-class event by severity, not a ledger entry to be discovered later.
3. **Freeze, don't fix, the affected record.** The report or audit-log segment in question is flagged (a status marker, never an in-place content edit) for manual forensic review; it is preserved exactly as found — not deleted, not renumbered, not backfilled — so a later medico-legal or compliance review can examine the exact broken state.
4. **Contain.** If the break correlates with a specific account (an amendment or sign event whose actor's session shows anomalous access patterns around the break), that account's active sessions are revoked (Permissions, below) as a precautionary containment step, pending investigation — this is an operational response, not an automated one; a human admin executes it.
5. **Downstream handling** (formal incident/compliance process, regulatory notification if applicable) is the practice's procedure, outside this handbook's scope; the platform's obligation ends at detect-loudly, never-silently-repair, and preserve-for-forensics.

**Known, accepted debt (CTO D3).** The audit chain is a single global linear structure — every write across every module and every clinic serializes on one advisory lock. For the current single-hospital Synology deployment this is the right tradeoff (adjudication principle 5: simplest operational story now, scale later) and the advisory-lock fix already lands the correctness half. Sharding or partitioning the chain per site is explicitly out of scope for this chapter and requires Chief Clinical Architect sign-off before any implementation, per Chapter 9's own flag on this exact point — multi-center scaling is a Chapter 15 (Future Roadmap) concern, not a near-term build item here.

### Permissions

**Role model.** Five conceptual roles the mandate names, mapped onto the as-built ERP role/permission system (`lib/db/src/schema/rolePermissions.ts`'s `ERP_ROLES`, `role_permissions` table, and `requireStaffAuth`/`requireStaffPermission`/`requireAdminRole` in `artifacts/api-server/src/middleware/requireStaffAuth.ts`):

| Conceptual role | ERP role / grant | What it can do in the reporting lifecycle |
|---|---|---|
| **Catalog author** | Any `radiologist`-role user granted `catalog:propose` in their per-user `permissions` array (Chapter 1's practice-editable surface: wording, tiles, combos, synonyms, aliases, rec phrasing, locale/practice overlays — D-05/§16.2) | Writes practice-wording **overlays** only (C-24) — never edits pack YAML or promotes an entry past `draft` status |
| **Clinical reviewer** | A `radiologist`-role user (or `admin`/`super_admin`) granted `catalog:approve` | Central sign-off on severity scales/order, scale↔management bindings, critical registry, lint-blocking rules, escalation behavior (D-05/§16.2); promotes catalog entries `draft → active`; the only role besides admin that can edit centrally-governed content |
| **Radiologist** | `radiologist` role, `role_permissions(radiologist, radiology).canFinalize = true` | Full read/write on drafts they hold the reading lock on; **Sign** (draft → signed); amend a signed report; view own reports and the provenance view for their own work |
| **Resident** | New `radiology_resident` ERP role (additive to `ERP_ROLES`, per D-37/B-23 — enum + one seeded `role_permissions` row, no retro change to any existing row), `canFinalize = false`, `canApprove = false` | Full read/write on drafts; **cannot** execute Sign or Amend under any circumstance (see countersign flow below) |
| **Admin** | `admin`/`super_admin` (`FULL_ACCESS_ROLES`) | Everything the above can do, plus: flag mutations (Chapter 8), critical-registry edits, catalog MAJOR-version governance events, chain-verification endpoints, tamper-response containment actions, granting `catalog:propose`/`catalog:approve` to specific users |

**Resident + countersign flow (decisive design — no source document specifies this; flagged in this chapter's open issues).** A resident drafts and edits a report normally. Because `radiology_resident.canFinalize` is hard-`false`, the resident cannot execute Sign — there is no "resident sign" event and no new state added to `DRAFT → SIGNED → AMENDED` (D-20 stays exactly as adjudicated; multi-stage review is not reintroduced). Instead, the resident marks the draft **"Ready for review"** — a DRAFT sub-label exactly like "Preliminary" (D-20's own precedent for labels that are not states) — which surfaces it in the supervising radiologist's review queue. The radiologist opens it, may edit freely, and executes the ordinary Sign action. Because authorship is always server-derived (Signed reports, above), `patient_reports.signedByName`/`signatureId` are always the radiologist's own identity, never the resident's — the single-signer invariant holds unmodified. The resident's contribution is preserved for medico-legal and teaching traceability using an existing column: `patient_reports.createdBy` is populated, at the draft's first save, from the creating session's identity — when `createdBy` names a `radiology_resident` and `signedByName` differs, the signed report is, by definition, a **countersigned resident report**; this fact is queryable directly from `patient_reports` without any new schema. No second signature event, no second hash, no second state — the countersign is fully expressed as *whose session created the draft* versus *whose session cleared the sign gate*.

**Hard role gates, not permission-array gates, for Sign and Amend.** This closes CTO §4.8's "granular RBAC dead code" finding (`canFinalize`/`canApprove` defined but only `canView` enforced) directly. `requireStaffPermission`'s per-user `permissions` string-array check must **never** be the sole gate for finalize or amend — an admin fat-fingering a permission string onto a `radiology_typist`'s account must not be able to grant sign authority. Instead, finalize and amend routes use a dedicated `requireFinalizeRole` middleware, built on the exact precedent `requireAdminRole` already establishes ("NOT toggleable via the per-user permissions array … regardless of permission configuration"): it checks `session.role` against the hard set `{"radiologist", "admin", "super_admin"}` directly, bypassing the `permissions` array entirely. `radiology_typist` and `radiology_resident` are excluded by role, permanently, independent of any Settings → Users configuration — this is the structural fix CTO §4.8 asks for, not a documentation promise.

**How this maps onto the as-built auth system.** `requireStaffAuth` authenticates a bearer token against `portal_sessions`, loads the user's `role` (normalized) and `permissions` (a JSON string array on `users`), and attaches both to `req.staffSession`; `role_permissions` (per role × module) is the admin-editable matrix that seeds a user's `permissions` array in the Settings → Users UI — it is not re-queried live on every request today, so any UI built for catalog-author/clinical-reviewer grants must write into the same per-user `permissions` array `requireStaffAuth` already reads (`catalog:propose`, `catalog:approve`), enforced via `requireStaffSubPermission("catalog", "…")`. `FULL_ACCESS_ROLES` (`admin`, `super_admin`) bypass every permission check, including the hard finalize gate above, by design. Add `"catalog"` to `PERMISSION_MODULES` (additive array edit — no migration) and seed default `role_permissions` rows for it (`radiologist`: `canView`/`canCreate`; `admin`/`super_admin`: everything) via an idempotent `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` feature migration (Chapter 9's sanctioned path).

**Session-token hardening (CTO §4.8).** `portal_sessions.token` is stored and compared in plaintext — a database dump leaks every live session token. Fix: add `portal_sessions.tokenHash` (new nullable text column, additive, unique-indexed), computed as `SHA256(token)`; issue the raw token to the client exactly once at login, and thereafter look sessions up by hashing the presented bearer token and comparing `tokenHash`. This ships as a dual-write migration (both columns populated on new sessions; existing plaintext-only sessions remain valid until they naturally expire — portal sessions are short-lived, so the plaintext column can be dropped in a follow-up migration shortly after cutover) via Chapter 9's sanctioned feature-migration path, not a schema rewrite.

**Per-action permission matrix.**

| Action | radiology_typist | radiology_resident | radiologist | clinical reviewer (`catalog:approve`) | admin / super_admin |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Create/edit DRAFT | — | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| Propose catalog overlay (`catalog:propose`) | — | — | ✓ (if granted) | ✓ | ✓ |
| Approve catalog entry `draft → active` | — | — | — | ✓ | ✓ |
| Edit critical registry / severity scales | — | — | — | — | ✓ |
| **Sign** (DRAFT → SIGNED) | — | — | ✓ | ✓ (as radiologist) | ✓ |
| **Amend** (post-sign addendum) | — | — | ✓ | ✓ (as radiologist) | ✓ |
| View own reports + own provenance | — | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| View any report + provenance (cross-radiologist) | — | — | — | ✓ | ✓ |
| View/verify audit hash chain | — | — | — | — | ✓ (or dedicated `audit:verify` grant) |
| Manage feature flags (Chapter 8) | — | — | — | — | ✓ |
| Revoke a session (tamper containment) | — | — | — | — | ✓ |

### Acceptance criteria

- Every sign, amend, catalog-governance, and flag-mutation action writes an `audit_logs` row whose `userId`/`userName`/`role` are server-derived from the authenticated session — zero client-supplied actor fields anywhere in these paths.
- `audit_logs` carries a unique index on `chain_hash`, DB-level append-only enforcement (`REVOKE`/trigger), and a `bigint` PK; `GET /api/audit-logs/verify` is reachable only by `admin`/`super_admin` or an `audit:verify` grant and returns `{ ok, checked, firstBrokenId, reason }`.
- `patient_reports.contentHash` is populated, in the same transaction as `catalogVersion`/`templateVersion`/`renderEngineVersion` and the `finalReportId` back-link, for every report signed after this chapter's adoption; no signed row exists with any of these four fields null.
- `report_amendments` inserts are rejected (409) when `priorVersionHash` does not chain to the prior link (the signed report's `contentHash` for amendment 1, the previous amendment's `newVersionHash` thereafter).
- `canFinalize`-gated actions (Sign, Amend) are enforced by a hard role check independent of the per-user `permissions` array; no Settings → Users configuration can grant `radiology_typist` or `radiology_resident` the ability to sign or amend.
- A resident-drafted, radiologist-signed report is queryable as such via `createdBy` vs `signedByName` without any additional schema, and no second sign event or state exists for it.
- Every rendered report element resolves, via `report_finding_instances` + Chapter 7's `ai_suggestion_audit` + `patient_reports`' version columns, to its catalog version and its human editor or AI suggestion id, for the full retention period, without depending on live catalog or model state.
- `portal_sessions` tokens are looked up by hash, not plaintext equality, for all sessions created after the hardening migration.
- A chain-verification failure never triggers an automatic repair; it always produces an `audit_logs` "tamper_detected" row and an out-of-band alert to admin/practice-owner within the same request/job that detected it.

### Build order

1. **Server-derived actor identity everywhere in this chapter's scope** — audit writes, sign, amend. Depends on nothing new; `requireStaffAuth`/`staffOf(req)` already exist and are the pattern to extend, not build.
2. **`requireFinalizeRole` hard gate** wired onto the finalize and amend routes, and removal of the legacy `peerReview.ts:85` client-supplied-`radiologistId` write path from any go-forward code (the legacy path itself stays frozen per D-20/D-27, simply not extended).
3. **`role_permissions`/`PERMISSION_MODULES` additions** — `"catalog"` module, `radiology_resident` ERP role, seeded default grants — via Chapter 9's idempotent feature-migration path.
4. **`patient_reports.contentHash` column + stamping write**, landed alongside the already-scheduled `catalogVersion`/`templateVersion`/`renderEngineVersion` stamping (D-18/C-02) and the `finalReportId` back-link wiring on the draft table.
5. **`report_amendments` chain-continuity validation** on the amend route.
6. **`audit_logs` hardening migration** (unique chain-hash index, append-only enforcement, bigint PK) — Chapter 9 item 3; this chapter's verification endpoint (step 7) is more useful once this lands but is not blocked by it.
7. **Chain-verification endpoint + content-hash verification utility**, wired into a nightly job and into the D-35 restore-verification job.
8. **Tamper-response wiring** (alert row + escalation-channel notification) — depends on 7 existing to produce something to alert on.
9. **`portal_sessions.tokenHash`** dual-write migration and lookup cutover.
10. **Report Provenance view** — read-only, depends on Chapter 3's `report_finding_instances` (already carries the needed columns) and Chapter 7's `ai_suggestion_audit` (already specified); no new write path.

This chapter depends on Chapter 3 (Finding Lifecycle) for `report_finding_instances`' provenance columns, Chapter 7 (AI Interaction Lifecycle) for `ai_suggestion_audit` and the AI half of the provenance view, Chapter 1 (Catalog Lifecycle) for the `draft/active/retired` status model that `catalog:approve` acts on, Chapter 8 (Feature Flags) for the flag-mutation audit events referenced above, and Chapter 9 (Database Evolution) for both sanctioned migration paths and the identity-strategy doctrine (D-37) this chapter's new tables/columns follow. Chapter 11 (Testing) must add coverage for exactly the paths the CTO review found untested — finalize, signed-report immutability, audit hash-chain, amendments, critical-finding escalation, Form-F (D-39) — before this chapter's build order above is considered complete, not after.

---

## Chapter 15 — Future roadmap

This chapter is the written home of the strand roadmap of record (D-03): everything in the platform's forward path that is not yet built, stated as trigger conditions and prerequisites rather than dates, so an engineer can tell at any moment whether a given piece of future work is buildable yet. It covers the nine areas the product and risk-governance sources actually project forward into — voice, AI, viewer, measurements, macros, structured reporting, FHIR, DICOM SR, and IHE — and for each states what exists today, the condition that makes investment justified, and the specific thing that must not be built ahead of that condition, because building it early either violates a BINDING safety rule elsewhere in this handbook or reproduces a mistake the CTO risk review already found once (`CTO_PRE_IMPLEMENTATION_RISK_REVIEW.md` §4, §7). Nothing here creates new obligations beyond what Chapters 1–14 already bind; this chapter sequences and gates, it does not re-litigate. Where a topic is genuinely unaddressed by any source document (IHE), that is stated plainly rather than invented as if it were adjudicated.

The table below orients the strands this chapter's scope touches; it is not the full flag registry — Chapter 8 (Feature Flag Rollout Plan) owns the complete, authoritative inventory of all thirteen `ff_radiology_*` flags and their rollout mechanics. Strands whose deep implementation belongs to another chapter (A, F → Chapter 2/1; G, N → Chapter 9/11) are referenced, not restated.

| Strand | Flag | Scope | Owning chapter for implementation | This chapter's mandate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| K | `ff_radiology_voice_structured` | Voice slot-filling into structured findings | Chapter 15 (this) | Voice |
| M | `ff_radiology_ai_assist` | AI-assisted suggestions with human confirmation gate | Chapter 7 (mechanics) / 15 (trigger) | AI |
| L | `ff_radiology_multiwindow` | Dockable multi-monitor workspace | Chapter 15 (this) | Viewer baseline |
| I | `ff_radiology_hierarchy` | Zone/Structure anatomic layers | Chapter 15 (this) | Viewer ROI-to-finding prerequisite |
| C | `ff_radiology_measurement_pool` | Finding-independent measurement pool, attach/detach | Chapter 5 | Measurements baseline (referenced) |
| D | `ff_radiology_classification` | Data-driven grading engine (TI-RADS/NIHSS-class) | Chapter 15 (this) | Measurements |
| H | `ff_radiology_catalog_delta` | Versioned per-modality catalog bundles + delta sync | Chapter 1 | Structured reporting / FHIR gate (referenced) |

### Voice

**Today.** Voice exists only as the legacy batch-transcription-queue page. It is explicitly not the target: the UX review names it "the wrong target" and the register directs it to receive no further investment (D-09). No command-grammar reuse and no dictation-to-structured mapping exist in the Reporting Workspace editor today.

**Target contract (definitive, D-09).** Editor-native push-to-talk: hold `Ctrl+Space` (or the equivalent pedal/dictaphone button — one identical input, per Chapter 12/ergonomics doctrine) dictates into the currently focused section. Dictated text lands as dictation-provisional (underlined amber) and clears only on edit or explicit section confirmation — never on dwell (C-12, BINDING, applies equally to voice as to AI ghost text). The command grammar is exactly ten verbs, no more, no fewer: *next field, previous field, impression, insert {macro}, accept, undo, park, sign, new paragraph, stop*. Voice "sign" hits the exact same sign gate as `Ctrl+Enter` — there is no separate, looser voice sign path. Dictation-to-structured mapping means: dictating over an unfilled template placeholder or a structured-shadow field (Chapter 3's parameter slots) fills it directly, with the same substantive-correction-stays-provisional rule as free text; the `insert {macro}` verb reuses the exact same macro-expansion mechanism the command line (`/`) uses (Chapter 4), not a parallel voice-only expansion path — one grammar, one implementation, two input methods.

**Trigger to invest.** The keyboard-first loop (P0/P1 releases: core keymap, one reporting surface, command-line unification) must already be live, because voice is a second input channel riding the same insertion mechanisms those releases build — command-grammar reuse is the whole point, and there is nothing to reuse until the command line and macro system it dictates into exist in their final form. Strand K (`ff_radiology_voice_structured`) is the vehicle; it does not unlock until its dependencies in the hands-release work are done.

**What must NOT be built early.**
- No new voice-specific UI surface. Voice output rides the four existing AI surfaces (ghost text, margin card, gutter mark, interrupt banner — Chapter 7); it does not get a fifth.
- No expansion of the transcription-queue page. It stays exactly as-is as the legacy long-form batch page; it is not the foundation to build PTT on top of.
- No agentic/free-grammar voice ("compare this with the 2024 study and draft the delta sentence") before the ten-verb PTT contract has demonstrated verb-recognition reliability in daily reading-room use. This is Future-tier and explicitly gated on measured voice-verb reliability, not on the ten-verb contract merely shipping (dependency 14b←14,1 in the AI feature map, Chapter 7).
- No relaxation of the "sign" verb's gate. A voice-triggered sign that skipped the critical-QA blocking list would be a distinct, worse regression from the keyboard path — it must not exist even transiently during development.

### AI (capabilities beyond Chapter 7)

Chapter 7 defines the eight laws, the four surfaces, and the twenty-feature map that governs every AI artifact today and through the P0–P2 releases. This section covers three specific extensions the mandate calls out that go beyond what Chapter 7 already fully specifies: making prior-comparison proactive rather than reactive, drafting at report level rather than sentence level, and how a model version is ever allowed to become the default.

**Proactive prior-comparison.** Today (Must tier, Feature 12, trust-chassis-adjacent) the delta brief renders when the radiologist opens a pre-staged study, in the Orient stage. The proactive extension is using the delta's magnitude as an input to the Zero-Click loop's next-best-study ordering — the same "AI-flagged urgency" tier the loop already reserves a slot for. This is not a new mechanism; it is Feature 16's Future-tier queue-reordering rule (§16, "pre-read image-AI critical flags may re-order the queue") generalized to interval-change magnitude instead of a single-image critical flag, and it inherits that rule's constraints exactly: any reorder is labeled on the served study's Orient brief (e.g., "Served early: lesion delta +40% since last study — verify independently"), never silent, and it can never suppress or bypass the independent read. **Trigger:** Feature 12 must already be in daily trusted use (12←10 dependency — trust chassis ships first, §4.4) and the reordering signal's false-flag rate must be measured and sit at a practice-acceptable level, the same gate class as image-level nudges (§17.2). It does not ship because it is possible; it ships because the measured precision earns it.

**Report-level drafting.** This must be built as a coordinated extension of the existing sentence-level ghosts (Feature 2 findings ghost, Feature 5 impression ghost), never as a new "write the report" mechanism. Concretely: an explicit, non-ambient invocation synthesizes a full candidate draft from study metadata, measurements, and (where available) image-derived observations, but still renders and is accepted exactly the way the individual ghosts are today — one finding at a time, one impression sentence at a time, `Tab`/`Shift+Tab`/type-over, each acceptance a single atomic undo step, nothing inserted without the gesture. The one boundary this feature must never cross is the standing refusal in §20d: an unstructured, freeform ask like "just write the whole report" returns a standing refusal with the reason, permanently. Report-level drafting is legitimate because it is a *coordinated, gesture-walked composition of already-trusted per-section ghosts*; it stops being legitimate the moment any part of it tries to skip the walk-through. **Trigger:** both underlying ghosts (Feature 2, Feature 5) must independently have reached the sustained sentence-accept target already tracked for them (>70% on routine studies, edit-after-accept falling — Part 3 target, Chapter 13) before they are composed into a joint draft; composing two ghosts that individually aren't trusted yet compounds the risk instead of amortizing it.

**Model upgrade policy.** `ai_model_routes` already does per-task routing (D-30); local-first is the default posture with cloud providers behind flags (D-31, BINDING); every suggestion is already pinned to a (prompt-template id+version, model/provider/version) tuple in the unconditional audit record (Chapter 7, D-32). The policy this chapter adds: **a new model version is never promoted to be a task's default route by direct edit.** It ships first as a non-default route, reachable only by explicit `/ask` opt-in or an admin-only override, and is evaluated in that shadow position against the audit-derived metrics the platform already collects — acceptance rate, edit-after-accept rate, override rate, and per-band calibration accuracy (B-10, D-22) — compared to the outgoing version over a minimum sample the AI governance owner sets. Only after that comparison clears does the route flip to make the new version the default; the flip is a data change (`ai_model_routes` row), not a code deploy. The same mechanism runs in reverse: a live version whose dashboard metrics regress (rising override rate, calibration drift) is pulled back to the prior version by the same route-flip, immediately, without a rollback deploy. This directly answers the open question in the risk-governance digest of how model-version regressions get caught — the dashboards already specified in §12.10/B-10 are the detection instrument; this policy is what acts on them.

**What must NOT be built early.**
- Any code path that flips a model-route default without the staged shadow-evaluation step, however "obviously better" the new version looks.
- Any report-level draft path that inserts more than one gesture-accepted unit at a time, or that accepts a freeform "write it for me" prompt as its trigger.
- Any queue-reorder signal that is not visibly labeled on the Orient brief of the study it moved.
- Building the Future-tier items already named and gated in the master spec (image-level missed-finding nudges, literature support, agentic voice) ahead of their named gates (§17.2) — those gates are restated in Chapter 7 and are not re-opened here.

### Viewer (deeper integration)

**Level 0 — today (as-built).** Viewer launch is fire-and-forget `window.open`; launch state lives in client `localStorage`; there is no viewer↔report reconciliation channel and no server-side "who viewed which images" audit (CTO §4.5 MED). The OHIF/Weasis measurement-import bridge has no producer — the only write path into `viewer_measurements` is a manual `POST` no client code calls (CTO §4.5 MED). This is the floor every level below builds on top of, not something this chapter re-designs.

**Level 1 — invisible launch.** Silent-resilient launch (§10.2): continuous background network-profile probing (LAN/VPN/public) from session start, not at click time; a diagnostic modal appears only for the genuinely unreachable case, in plain language, with one recommended action pre-focused. Paired with the paired-window covenant baseline (§10.1): study-follow (the viewer advances automatically when the loop advances), a shared identity band with the same per-patient accent color in both windows, `F2` flips focus, signing never yanks focus to the viewer, and the viewer never raises itself. This level is substantially specified already by the UX/master-spec sources and is scoped into the loop and calm releases; it is stated here only as the prerequisite baseline for Levels 2–3, not as new roadmap content.

**Level 2 — bidirectional context.** The viewer stops being something merely launched *at* and starts exchanging live state with the report over the same channel the paired-window covenant already requires. Transport is decided, not left open (the source UX review left it a named open point between `BroadcastChannel` and a shared worker; this chapter closes it consistent with the register's simpler-single-box-story principle): **`BroadcastChannel` is the transport for the OHIF case**, where the viewer is a same-origin browser tab the report window can reach directly with no server hop and no extra worker process to deploy, version, or debug on the NAS. Weasis is a native desktop process, not a browser context, and cannot join a `BroadcastChannel`; for a Weasis-paired session, the same two payloads (cursor-to-series, on-screen-series-back) are carried instead by short-interval polling against the server-side viewer-session endpoint that this level's "who viewed" audit event already requires (point (b) below) — this reuses one server surface instead of standing up a second transport for the native case. Two concrete uses justify building this channel: (a) the report can tell the viewer which finding/series the cursor is currently in, so the viewer can bring up the relevant series/frame — this is the missing dependency behind Chapter 7's explainability contract, which already promises an evidence view that can highlight "image region outlined in paired viewer" (Feature 10) but has no channel today to make that happen; (b) the viewer can report back which series/frame/window is on screen, which becomes the first real server-side "who viewed which images" audit event, replacing the current localStorage-only launch record and directly answering the CTO §4.5 finding. **Trigger:** Level 1 stable in production. **What this level must not do:** it must not let the viewer window raise itself, steal focus from the editor, or resize/move the report canvas — the paired-window covenant's focus discipline (`F2`-only flip) and the report canvas's "never moves, resizes, or scrolls due to panel activity" invariant (Chapter 7/UX doctrine) apply to this channel exactly as to everything else; a bidirectional channel is not license to relax either guarantee.

**Level 3 — ROI-to-finding.** The frontier item: a radiologist marks a region in the paired viewer (an OHIF/Weasis measurement or annotation tool) and that region becomes a *candidate* — a measurement-pool row (Chapter 5; the pool's `source` enum already reserves `ohif`/`weasis` values for exactly this) or a candidate finding location keyed against the Zone/Structure anatomic hierarchy (Strand I, `ff_radiology_hierarchy`, which this level depends on to know which structure a given ROI belongs to). Offer-never-act applies without exception: a viewer-drawn ROI proposes a pool row or a finding location; it is never auto-attached to a finding or auto-inserted into report text. The AI-initiated mirror of this same capability is Feature 4b (image-level missed-finding detection, Future tier, Chapter 7) — both share one gate: image-AI/ROI precision at a practice-acceptable false-nudge rate, localizing only ("look here"), never diagnosing, and rendered only before sign-off, never during active reading. **Trigger:** Level 2's reconciliation channel must exist first and the measurement-import bridge must have a real producer — building ROI-to-finding on top of a bridge with no producer would recreate the exact CTO §4.5 defect one layer higher, with a UI in front of it this time instead of behind it.

**What must NOT be built early.**
- Four-monitor sprawl or detachable/floating ("tearable") panels — these are named deliberate non-goals (§6.3/§10.3), not deferred features; they are not on this roadmap at any level.
- Level 3 work before Level 2's producer-backed reconciliation channel exists.
- Any viewer-originated write that bypasses the attach gesture Chapter 5 already defines for every pool row, regardless of source.

### Measurements

Chapter 5 owns the measurement pool's shape and lifecycle (Strand C) — that is the baseline this section extends, not restates.

**Auto-measurement expansion.** The fixed auto-calc set (Evans index, ABC/2, midline shift, CTR, prostate volume, PVR, Meyerding, nodule average diameter, CT severity score, ABI bands, reflux duration — D-10) is not the ceiling; new formulas are expected over time as new studies and shared-library entries are added. The rule for how they are added is already ratified by D-08's resolution of the canal-stenosis defect and generalizes to every future case: a new severity-from-measurement mapping enters as a `derives:` rule on the shared library, with clinical-owner sign-off, and it may power at most a *suggested* grade ghost (offer-never-act) — never an asserted grade written by application code. **What must NOT be built early:** any new numeric threshold hardcoded into `radiologySmartEngine.ts` or an equivalent — this is precisely the mistake D-08 already found and reversed once; it must not recur under a different scale's name.

**Classification/grading engine.** Strand D (`ff_radiology_classification`) is the generic data-driven grading engine for TI-RADS/NIHSS-class scales — scales with multi-factor scoring rules more complex than a single ordered severity ladder. **Trigger:** the next content tranche that needs one. USG Thyroid/Neck (already named as next-tranche content, master spec §14/§17.3) is the first study that requires TI-RADS and is therefore the forcing function for Strand D; it should not be built generically in advance of a study that needs it, and it must not be built as a one-off bespoke engine for that single study — a per-study bespoke grading path repeats the CTO's "12+ parallel measurement tables, no canonical entity" mistake at the grading-engine layer instead of the storage layer. Every future *-RADS scale entering through Strand D follows the same mandatory pattern already fixed for BI-RADS: trigger lives on the scale, routing lives on the registry (D-06), never duplicated.

**Lesion tracking maturity.** Today, a manual Tumor Follow-up panel exists as-built. The target (AI spec Feature 13, Should tier) is a per-lesion story — sparkline of size/measurement across all prior studies, target-lesion status, an applicable response-criterion chip (RECIST-style) — with two hard constraints that never relax as this matures: lesion identity linking across studies is always an explicit human act, AI may only *propose* a match, never silently link one; and any tracked lesion left unaddressed at sign-off raises a ◌ completeness mark (Chapter 7 owns the mark mechanics; this chapter states only that lesion tracking is a source of completeness marks, not a new lint category). **Trigger:** Feature 12 (prior comparison) must already be in daily trusted use — 13 depends on 12 in the feature dependency graph, and 12 itself is gated behind the trust chassis (§4.4). **What must NOT be built early:** any lesion auto-link that fires without a confirm gesture, and any lesion-tracking auto-insert of a longitudinal sentence at any confidence tier — D-21's no-auto-insert rule is not scale-specific; it applies to a longitudinal ghost exactly as it applies to a single extracted value.

### Macros

**Today.** Three macro-adjacent table lineages exist. The canonical, actively developed store is `radiology_text_macros` plus `radiology_smart_macros` for measurement-aware macros (D-25); `radiologist_macros` is legacy and frozen. A separate, earlier, unconsolidated attempt at personal/shareable content — `radiology_personal_templates`, `radiology_template_packs`, `radiology_template_favorites` — also exists in the schema and is frozen read-only under the same legacy-template freeze that governs the rest of the template-table family (D-29). Any future macro evolution builds on the canonical `radiology_text_macros`/`radiology_quick_favorites` pair; it does not resurrect or extend the frozen personal-template/template-pack tables, which represent a design the platform has already tried once and superseded.

**Evolution target — shareable, versioned personal content coexisting with the governed catalog.** Three maturity levels, additive at each step:

1. **Personal (today).** A private, per-creator, unversioned `/`-namespace text expansion, expanded via the D-25 canonical store.
2. **Versioned personal.** Each edit to a personal macro appends a new version row rather than overwriting in place — the same additive-only doctrine (B-23) already governs every other hot-table extension in this handbook, applied here to a purely personal object. This requires no governance review, because a versioned personal macro never touches the governed catalog; it is a private history, not a catalog change.
3. **Shareable.** A radiologist may publish a personal macro to their practice as a nomination. This is not a new governance mechanism — it is the existing quarterly content-governance loop (B-27: "repeated free text → draft tile candidates, AI proposes, human editor decides") with a manual trigger added alongside the automatic quarterly nomination: a radiologist-initiated "propose to catalog" action. Publishing a macro converts it into a governed-catalog *candidate*; it never becomes a live tile or recommendation by that act alone. D-05 (BINDING) is unconditional here: the critical registry, severity scales, and their bindings are never reachable through this path at all, and even ordinary tiles/recommendations reach the live catalog only with clinical sign-off, regardless of how a candidate was nominated.

**Trigger to invest.** Level 1 already ships. Levels 2–3 are additive schema/UI work on an existing table, not an architectural unlock — the trigger is demand, not a blocked dependency: sustained macro-usage adoption (the platform already tracks a >40% macro-usage target) plus at least one practice explicitly requesting cross-radiologist sharing.

**What must NOT be built early.**
- A second governance track that lets a shared personal macro reach the live catalog without clinical sign-off — this would violate D-05 directly.
- Treating personal-macro version history as if it were the catalog's own semver (B-03) — they are different objects with different governance, and conflating them would let personal edits masquerade as catalog changes in analytics or audit.
- Any revival of `radiologist_macros`, `radiology_personal_templates`, or `radiology_template_packs` as storage for this work.

### Structured reporting (full structured-first reporting maturity model)

This is the section every FHIR/DICOM SR/IHE decision below depends on, and it must be read against D-19 (BINDING) without exception: `patient_reports.body` is THE authoritative, append-only, content-hashed signed record at every level of this ladder. Nothing below ever inverts that. What matures is *where authoring starts*, never *where legal authority lives*.

- **Level 0 — today (as-built).** Prose is authored directly in the editor. `report_finding_instances` (Strand A) exists and is dual-written behind `ff_radiology_structured_core`, but `structured_json` on drafts and on signed reports is an explicitly regenerated cache — never a source of truth (B-22, D-19) — and the render engine (F1a/F1b) is a pure state-transition facade over the same prose fields, not a structure-to-prose generator yet.
- **Level 1 — structured entry, generated prose.** Once Strand A's dual-write is complete and Strand F's render engine becomes the sole text producer (both already-named strands whose deep mechanics belong to Chapter 1/2, not restated here), structured findings become the *input* prose is deterministically generated from at every render, rather than free-hand prose with structure captured as an afterthought. The report a radiologist reads and edits is still prose; what changed is that the prose is now provably derived from the structure underneath it, not merely accompanied by a best-effort structured snapshot. `patient_reports.body` remains what gets signed and remains authoritative.
- **Level 2 — catalog delta reaches live authoring.** Once structured entry is the default authoring path, Strand H (`ff_radiology_catalog_delta`, Chapter 1's territory) is what lets a governed catalog update change the choices available to *new* findings without ever touching an already-signed report — D-18/C-02's snapshot-at-sign discipline (catalog/template/render-engine versions stamped on `patient_reports` in the same transaction as the sign) already defines how a signed report freezes the versions it used; this level cannot start until that stamping is actually wired (it is currently unwired — the register notes it as a pending Strand F/H ticket).
- **Level 3 — canonical coded entity (Future, gated).** A single canonical coded finding/measurement entity underneath the structured layer — typed value, coded concept (LOINC/SNOMED/RadLex), coded unit (UCUM), method, confidence, provenance — per CTO recommendation C2. This is not scaffolding for interoperability exports; it *is* the interoperability foundation, and nothing in the FHIR or DICOM SR sections below may be attempted for a given finding class before that class has a Level 3 coded representation.

**What must NOT be built early.**
- Any change that makes `structured_json` or any structured store authoritative ahead of `patient_reports.body` — this inverts D-19 and is prohibited outright, at every maturity level, with no exception carved out for "structured-first."
- A coded-vocabulary layer bolted onto individual modalities one at a time with no shared entity — this repeats the CTO's "12+ disjoint measurement tables" mistake at the coding layer; Level 3 lands as one canonical entity across modalities or it does not start.
- Coding the whole catalog against LOINC/SNOMED speculatively, with no consumer waiting on it — the CTO's own cost table prices this class of work at 12–36 engineering-months and calls it "partially unrecoverable" (D2); it is gated on a named trigger (see FHIR/DICOM SR below), never built ahead of one.

### FHIR

**Today.** No FHIR support exists. No coded vocabulary exists anywhere in the platform (CTO T2). The CTO verdict on DICOM SR/FHIR/HL7 export is 🟠, explicitly "lossy-by-construction" given the current data model.

**What FHIR export means, precisely.** A `DiagnosticReport` resource per signed `patient_reports` row, referencing `Observation` resources for each coded finding/measurement — sourced only from the Structured reporting Level 3 canonical entity above, never generated by parsing prose with NLP as a substitute for real coding. `DiagnosticReport.status` may only represent a report that has actually reached SIGNED or AMENDED in this handbook's state machine (D-20); there is no FHIR export of a DRAFT.

**Trigger to invest.** A real, named external consumer: a referral hospital's EHR, a government interoperability mandate (e.g., an ABDM-class requirement in the Indian regulatory context), or an insurance/TPA integration that specifically requires FHIR. It is not built speculatively. Structured reporting Level 3 must already exist for the finding/measurement classes that consumer needs — coding one study type at a time as real demand arrives is the acceptable incremental path; coding everything up front on the chance it will someday be needed is the D2 cost-table mistake this handbook explicitly warns against.

**What must NOT be built early.** An export endpoint that serializes prose into `Observation.valueString` as a stand-in for real coding. This is worse than no export at all: it produces a resource that *looks* FHIR-compliant to a receiving system while carrying none of the queryability a coded Observation promises, inviting a downstream consumer to trust data that cannot support the query it appears to support. This is the literal shape of the CTO's "lossy-by-construction" finding, and building it is how that finding stops being theoretical.

### DICOM SR

**Ingest maturity.** Today, incoming SR concept codes are discarded on arrival (CTO §4.10); the default automated measurement source is OCR (`usg_measurements.source` defaults `'ocr'`), not SR, even on modalities that already emit structured Doppler/echo measurement objects. The maturity ladder:

1. **Today:** SR objects arrive and their concept codes are dropped.
2. **Near-term (additive, Chapter 5's territory, does not require Level 3):** SR-sourced values populate the measurement pool with `source = dicom_sr` and use the coded-concept field the pool row already reserves (Chapter 5) — this is parsing work on an already-defined shape, not a redesign, and it can start independently of the Structured reporting Level 3 gate because the pool's coded-concept slot exists precisely to receive it without waiting.
3. **Mature:** once SR ingest is proven reliable for a modality, a coded SR value naturally earns a higher confidence band than an OCR extraction of the same quantity through the existing confidence-band and provenance machinery (Chapters 5, 7) — no new mechanism is needed for this, only correct source tagging on ingest.

**Export.** `dicom_sr_export_queue` already exists in the schema as an inert stub (CTO §6). Export maturity gates identically to FHIR: it requires the Level 3 canonical coded entity for whatever finding/measurement classes are exported, and a named consumer — a receiving PACS/viewer or a referring site's archive that specifically requests SR rather than a rendered PDF. Given the single-clinic Synology deployment this handbook is written for (register principle 5: prefer the simpler single-hospital operational story), DICOM SR export currently has no consumer and sits below FHIR export in priority unless a specific PACS integration names it.

**What must NOT be built early.**
- Wiring the existing `dicom_sr_export_queue` stub to actually enqueue and silently drop items before a real consumer exists — an inert stub that starts silently failing is a worse failure mode than an honestly-inert one, the same false-success pattern D-36 already had to fix once for backups.
- Making SR ingest the sole or mandatory measurement source before OCR and manual entry are proven to cover the same fields on every shipped study — this would regress fields today reachable only via OCR from legacy machine consoles that never emit SR.

### IHE

No source document in this repository — the master design spec, the seed spec, the UX/AI experience specs, the roadmap/architecture documents, or the CTO risk review — names any IHE profile, actor, or transaction. This section is therefore not an adjudicated register decision; it is stated here as the chapter owner's minimal placeholder under the register's operating principle to prefer the simplest single-hospital story, pending a real trigger. Treat it as advisory scaffolding, not a specification to build against yet.

**Today.** No IHE actor is implemented or scoped anywhere in the platform.

**What compliance would require, if triggered.** At minimum: conformance to Scheduled Workflow (SWF), which mostly formalizes what the existing PACS/RIS worklist exchange (`radiology_worklist`/MWL) already does ad hoc; and, if a referral network requires cross-site study or report exchange, Cross-Enterprise Document Sharing for Imaging (XDS-I.b) or a national equivalent. Either requires a written conformance/integration statement and passing a receiving site's or vendor's interoperability test — this is a formal compliance cost as well as a code cost, and must be budgeted as both.

**Trigger to invest.** A specific multi-site referral network, teleradiology partner, or hospital-network procurement requirement names an IHE profile as a contractual condition. Absent a named requirement, no IHE work is scoped — the same named-consumer gate that governs FHIR and DICOM SR export above applies here, for the same reason.

**What must NOT be built early.** Any IHE actor implementation before both a named triggering consumer exists and Structured reporting Level 3 is in place for the content being exchanged. IHE profiles such as XDS-I.b assume coded, queryable content at the far end; building the transport/integration layer before the content layer is coded reproduces the same lossy-by-construction failure this chapter already rules out for premature FHIR and SR export.

### Acceptance criteria

- The ten-verb voice command grammar (D-09) is implemented once, shared identically between keyboard, command-line, and voice invocation; no separate voice-only insertion mechanism exists.
- The legacy transcription-queue page receives zero new features after this chapter is adopted; any voice work lands in the editor-native PTT contract instead.
- No model-route default (`ai_model_routes`) is ever flipped without a logged staged-evaluation comparison against the outgoing version's acceptance/edit/override/calibration metrics.
- Any report-level AI draft, if built, is verified to reject a freeform "write the whole report" invocation with the standing refusal, and to require a gesture per accepted unit with no bulk-accept path.
- Any AI-driven queue reorder carries a visible, specific label on the reordered study's Orient brief; none is silent.
- No viewer-originated ROI, measurement, or region marker is ever attached to a finding or inserted into report text without an explicit attach gesture.
- A server-side viewer session/"who viewed" audit record exists (replacing the localStorage-only launch record) before any ROI-to-finding work begins.
- No new auto-calculated measurement or severity-from-measurement mapping ships without a `derives:` shared-library entry carrying clinical-owner sign-off; none is hardcoded in application code.
- Every lesion-identity link across studies in the audit trail has a recorded confirming human gesture; none is system-generated without one.
- Every macro published from personal to shared status is traceable in the governance-nomination log; none reaches the live catalog without the clinical sign-off the ordinary tile/recommendation path already requires.
- At every structured-reporting maturity level exercised in production, `patient_reports.body` — never `structured_json` or any other structured store — is confirmed as the row a legal/medico-legal export or printout is generated from.
- No FHIR `Observation`/`DiagnosticReport`, DICOM SR export, or IHE transaction exists in code without a named external consumer recorded against it in this chapter's future revisions.

### Build order

1. **Voice (Strand K):** depends on the P1 hands-release command-line/macro unification (Chapter 4/8) already being live; build the PTT contract and ten-verb grammar only after that dependency ships.
2. **AI capabilities (Strand M + model-upgrade policy):** depends on the trust chassis (Chapter 7, features 1/9/10/19/20) already being in daily use; proactive prior-comparison and report-level drafting each additionally depend on their underlying Must-tier features (12, and 2+5 respectively) independently clearing their trust thresholds first.
3. **Viewer Level 2 (bidirectional context):** depends on Level 1 (invisible launch + paired-window covenant baseline, largely a loop/calm-release deliverable) being stable in production. Viewer Level 3 (ROI-to-finding) depends on Level 2's reconciliation channel and Strand I (Zone/Structure hierarchy) both existing, and on Chapter 5's measurement pool already having a real producer.
4. **Measurements:** auto-measurement expansion has no hard blocker beyond the `derives:` governance path (Chapter 3) and can proceed incrementally at any time; the classification engine (Strand D) should be built generically no earlier than the first next-tranche study that actually requires it (USG Thyroid/Neck, TI-RADS); lesion tracking maturity depends on Feature 12 (prior comparison) being trusted in daily use.
5. **Macros:** versioning and sharing depend only on the already-shipped D-25 canonical store and on measured demand; no architectural blocker.
6. **Structured reporting:** Level 1 depends on Strand A/F completing (Chapter 1/2's territory); Level 2 depends on D-18's version-stamping-at-sign wiring landing; Level 3 depends on a named FHIR or DICOM SR consumer existing (see 7 below) — it is not started speculatively.
7. **FHIR / DICOM SR export:** both depend on Structured reporting Level 3 for the specific finding/measurement classes involved, and each independently depends on a named external consumer; DICOM SR ingest maturity (near-term tier) is the one item in this chapter with no upstream blocker and may proceed on its own schedule as additive work in Chapter 5's measurement pool.
8. **IHE:** depends on both a named contractual trigger and the relevant content already being coded per Structured reporting Level 3 — the last item in this chapter's build order by construction, never started ahead of its trigger.

---
